Chapter 3

Firing Group

     3-17. Performance of the direct fire engagements portion of the STX (firing group) is
     on the crew’s vehicle. Table design is set up to train and evaluate the crew’s ability to
     engage stationary or moving targets placed in a tactical array, during day and limited
     visibility conditions. Crews execute these tasks from the offense and defense.

     3-18. The engagements may be full-scale or scaled, depending on the usable area within
     the training area. Figure 3-6 on page 63 lists the requirements for the 10 specific
     engagements. Crew’s target acquisition skills and engagement techniques, using
     3/4-scale targets, is challenged. See TC 25-8 for additional information on target
     dimensions for scaled ranges.

     3-19. All engagements are evaluated using certified VCE’s using DA Form 8265 and
     DA Form 8265-1 as the official record of training. Master Gunners must ensure the
     training records are included in each crew’s gunnery packet.

     3-20. The engagements listings are by capability type using the standard engagement
     naming structure listed in TC 3-20.31. It provides the requirements for each engagement,
     which are described in paragraphs 3-21 through 3-27.

Task Description–Table III

     3-21. The task description includes both the engagement number (the table number plus
     engagement task) followed by the system capability trained.

Firing Vehicle Posture–Table III

     3-22. The firing vehicle posture establishes the offense or defense condition for the
     engagement.

Firer–Table III

     3-23. The firer column identifies which crewmember is to engage the target listed.
     Targets defeated by a different crewmember listed as the firer result in an auto-zero
     penalty, failing to adhere to course conditions.

Weapon (or Ammunition)–Table III

     3-24. The weapon or ammunition column identifies the weapon (coaxial, or primary
     armament ammunition type) that is used to defeat the threat. Design of each engagement
     is for a specific weapon or ammunition type (primary armament) for use, which relates
     to the crew conduct of fire, optic selection, engagement techniques, or other unique
     implied tasks. Crews that fail to announce the appropriate weapon or ammunition, or
     appropriate selection for subsequent or supplemental engagements may be penalized
     IAW TC 3-20.31.

Target Type–Table III

     3-25. The target type is the target required for the presentation, IAW TC 25-8.

60  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                     Table III–Situational Training Exercise

Target Posture–Table III

     3-26. The target posture establishes if the target is stationary, moving, or evasive for the
     engagement. See TC 3-20.31 for more information on target postures.

Range (to Target)–Table III

     3-27. The required range (full scale or equivalent scaled range) from the firing vehicle
     to the target. For offensive engagements, this distance calculation is from the center of
     the movement box to the target. The range to target distance is provided in meters, and
     may be displayed as a whole number distance, (for example, 600). The target may be
     placed at the distance indicated, plus or minus 200 meters. For example, the target may
     be placed between 400 and 800 meters. No change to the engagement modifier (EM) is
     authorized.

Engagement Modifier–Table III

     3-28. The EM is the time in seconds required to achieve 100 points against the listed
     threat. To defeat the threat successfully, the crew must achieve the kill standard against
     the target and have an overall engagement time of no more than the EM plus 20 seconds.
     See TC 3-20.31 for the evaluation, timing, and scoring procedures.

Missile Time of Flight–Table III

     3-29. The missile time of flight section identifies the time of flight for the selected
     missile based on range to target. This time starts when the antitank weapons effect
     signature simulator (ATWESS), charge is fired (trigger squeeze plus 1 second built-in
     test) and ends when a live missile would arrive at the target location. This time is used
     by the target operator to trigger effects on target through means of a BES and lower the
     target presentation that has been properly engaged by the crew. During this time the firer
     of the missile must execute and maintain proper target tracking techniques as assessed
     by the VCE.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  61

Chapter 3

Scenario Requirements

     3-30. Units may not deviate from any target type, target posture, range, and weapon
     system/ammunition type for Table III. Firing may be in any sequence that best supports
     their training area, flow-of the range, and time available.

     3-31. The unit options and requirements are that the—
          • Unit determines how many engagements are conducted during the day phase
                and night phase.
          • Unit determines the sequence of the engagements.
          • Unit determines which tasks are conducted during the day and night phase.
          • Unit determines which engagements will include CBRN conditions. Units must
                select a minimum of one day and one night engagement conducted in a
                chemical environment.
          • BESs are used as described in TC 3-20.31 for all vehicle target panel
                presentations.
          • Target panels (all) are 3/4 scale at full range distances or scaled accordingly if
                using a scaled training range or training area.
          • Unit may use F-type troop targets in lieu of E-type silhouettes to increase
                training for target detection, acquisition, and reticle aim.
          • Units use available force-on-target training devices or TADSS.
                 Laser-based systems.
                 Laser-target interface devices (LTIDs).
                 Additional TADSS use are at the discretion of the unit commander.
          • Safety tasks (all) must be conducted day and night.
          • Call-for tasks (all) must be conducted day and night.
          • One TOW missile engagement is conducted day and night.

     3-32. Additional information concerning the engagements, the terms of reference, or the
     layout for each event’s scenario can be found in TC 3-20.31. (See figure 3-6 for the
     engagements for the firing group.)

Chapter 3

Instrumentable–Multiple Integrated Laser Engagement System, CVTESS

     3-43. The instrumentable-multiple integrated laser engagement system combat vehicle
     tactical engagement simulation system (I-MILES CVTESS) is a laser-based training
     device to for use on Abrams, Bradley, medium tank, and opposing forces tanks and
     fighting vehicles to provide real time casualty effects. I-MILES CVTESS is an
     evolutionary approach for replacing older systems with equipment that provides better
     training fidelity. It will reinforce crew duties, reward proper engagement techniques,
     and develop tactical maneuver skills of armor and mechanized infantry combined arms
     teams up to brigade level. It provides unit commanders an integrated training system in
     force-on-force and force-on-target training events at home station training areas through
     instrumented training. The system interfaces with instrumentation systems at combat
     training centers.

Laser Target Interface Device

     3-44. For use with I-MILES CVTESS or other laser-based training systems when the
     unit is conducting force-on-target training, the LTIDs provides the appropriate hit
     recording at the target emplacement and when stimulated, signals the target lifting
     mechanism to record a hit. This is a required device on Table III.

Home Station Instrumentation Training System

     3-45. Units are encouraged to integrate the home station/Army mobile instrumentation
     training system (HITS). This system will allow for the highest fidelity AARs for the
     crew’s engagements. The HITS provides exercise management, monitoring, and control.
     It includes the ability to provide training performance feedback through onsite AARs.

     3-46. HITS is a transportable system able to enhance live training at home stations up
     to the battalion level. HITS provides instrumentation through automated tools for unit
     leaders and designated OC/Ts to give performance feedback to training units. AARs and
     take-home packages can provide training performance feedback. As part of voice
     communications, HITS accepts single-channel ground and airborne radio systems radio
     transmissions.

Crew Module Unit/Crew Module Unit Recorder–Table III

     3-47. The crew module unit (CMU)/integrated player unit (IPU)/crew module unit
     recorder (CMUR) is an advanced eight channel audio/video recording device for combat
     vehicles. The CMU/IPU/CMUR can simultaneously record up to eight channels of
     video, one channel of audio, and has the capability to monitor dual 1,553 data busses.
     The CMU/IPU/CMUR is equipped with a Global Positioning System that captures the
     vehicle’s position location information. The CMU/IPU is for use on a digital range
     complex whereas the CMUR does not require any external support. The CMUR has an
     integrated AAR suite and stores information on a solid-state drive. The AAR suite
     uploads the data from the solid-state drive allowing a full AAR presentation.

PREPARE FOR TABLE III

     3-48. Units execute the crew STX in a variety of ways. Typically, each platoon conducts
     training independently prior to the crew STX event, either in the motor pool, simulator

70         TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

              Table III–Situational Training Exercise

     system, or in a local training area. The primary trainers during the crew STX are the
     company commander with OC/Ts providing oversight and assistance as necessary.

TRAIN THE TRAINERS–TABLE III

     3-49. The primary trainers during the crew STX are the company commander with
     OC/Ts providing oversight and assistance as necessary.

             Note. Much like crew platform evaluators, the OC/Ts for any crew exercise
             provide exceptional training for the OC/T as a leader, second only to
             executing the task with their organization. Units should dedicate sufficient
             time to OC/T certification to leaders who can provide experienced
             mentorship, with the complete understanding that their OC/T task is also
             exceptional training for them.

     3-50. Units provide the following to prepare the trainers:
          • Commander’s training objectives.
          • Scenario and lane concept with crew tasks.
          • Conduct of the range briefing for the training event.
          • Review of T&EOs and evaluation process for all the tasks.
          • Risk assessment.
          • Identification of supporting doctrinal publications for use as primary
                references.
          • TADSS, pyrotechnics, blanks, and BESs used during the training event
                (see appendix A for the training munitions requirements for the complete
                training strategy).

ENGAGEMENT TASK AND PURPOSE–TABLE III

     3-51. The crews, leaders, and evaluators must understand the task and purpose of the
     engagements, the critical skills tasks, and how they are linked to their training. Each of
     the engagements should be reviewed to understand what they contain, the standards for
     each, and how they build from previous training and to the next event’s engagements.

RECONNAISSANCE OF THE SITE–TABLE III

     3-52. Commanders use an authorized training area or series of training areas for the
     Table III, STX. Commanders follow the installation’s policies and procedures for
     scheduling the training location as far in advance as practical. Once commanders have
     established the method of training and evaluation, they should secure the training
     location and appropriate TADSS, equipment, or systems that enhance the training event.

     3-53. Once scheduled, the commander, Master Gunner, platoon leaders, and platoon
     sergeants conduct a reconnaissance of the training areas to—

          • Verify sufficient space is available to support the entire unit through multiple
                training lanes.

          • Ensure multiple iterations or rotations through the scenario can be achieved
                with appropriate retraining opportunities.

          • Ensure sufficient administrative areas are available to support group
                discussions, briefings, and AARs.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  71


---

Chapter 3

          • Provide sand table or terrain board.
          • Provide terrain board kits.
          • Verify the training area can support the unit’s training while in a chemical

                environment, based on the scenario design (protection).
          • Verify casualty evacuation and medical evacuation notional capabilities in

                support of the training scenario; for which units mostly include only—
                 Frequency modulation (FM) reporting.
                 Request procedures for training purposes (sustainment).

ISSUE THE ORDER–TABLE III

     3-54. Commanders can issue an operation order (OPORD), warning order
     (WARNORD), or use the training schedule as necessary. The WARNORD should
     include the appropriate tasks, required leaders, and equipment needed, as well as the
     unit’s training timeline including walk-through, rehearsals, and AARs. WARNORDs
     for external organizations must include sufficient reaction time for the appropriate
     personnel and equipment needed.

     3-55. The OPORD or WARNORD should include the list of tasks that commanders
     evaluate during the event, and the method of evaluation. Commanders should provide
     sufficient time for subordinate leaders to train the tasks and skills listed in the order.
     Commanders should ensure the appropriate training resources are available and
     accessible. Upon receipt, subordinate leaders that have SOPs for their organizations
     must ensure their procedures support and complement the higher headquarters
     publication.

REHEARSE–TABLE III

     3-56. The unit uses all training resources, materials, and equipment available to conduct
     rehearsals of training events. During the rehearsal, commanders ensure their unit has
     sufficient time to train to the specified standard including AARs and retraining time.
     Commanders ensure their unit verifies optional scenarios that increase complexity when
     warranted.

     3-57. Commanders use the rehearsal as an opportunity to verify the tasks selected for
     Table III are consistent with the commander’s training guidance.

     3-58. Units should rehearse the training event from start to finish, and confirm the
     following:

          • The opposing forces understand the training scenarios.
          • Sufficient space is available to provide appropriate distances for offensive

                engagements.
          • Analog and digital systems are operational within the organization and to the

                higher headquarters regardless of their location.
          • Additional OC/T training support packages are complete and provide for

                multiple iterations.
          • Sufficient administrative areas are available.
          • A review is performed of AAR capabilities.
          • Methods and actions required to provide fire support (notional).

72  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                     Table III–Situational Training Exercise

          • Integration of attack aviation and close air support during the scenario (notional
                inclusion for call-for tasks).

          • Location of retraining for crews is available as necessary.

     3-59. Once the unit completes the walk-through and training rehearsal, the commander
     makes any necessary adjustments to the plan and issues fragmentary orders
     (FRAGORDs) as necessary.

EXECUTE TABLE III

     3-60. Once the unit arrives at the training location, they should—
          • Conduct a safety briefing for the training event.
          • Provide a conduct of the range briefing to establish the sequence of events for
                the training day.
          • Conduct sensitive item checks prior to executing training.
          • Assign OC/Ts as appropriate.
          • Issue the OPORD for the training mission.
          • Execute the training scenario.

     3-61. The training unit conducts informal AARs of each crew completing a phase of
     training within the training mission. During training, the assigned OC/T, executive
     officer, first sergeant (1SG), and Master Gunner review the subordinate unit’s
     performance. They follow the unit SOP, prescribed tactics, techniques, and procedures
     (TTP), and the appropriate T&EOs, and review completed crew score sheets and roll-up
     documentation for clarity and completeness.

     3-62. The unit’s scenario sequence will vary based on the training area available, time,
     day/night mix of engagements, and determination of which day and night engagements
     include operating in a chemical environment. (See figure 3-13 on page 74 through
     figure 3-32 on page 93 for the task, conditions, and standards for each engagement
     sequentially.)

Chapter 3

ASSESS TABLE III TRAINING EVENT

     3-73. The intent of STXs is to train the crews within the organization on the appropriate
     conduct of fire skills, various crew critical skills, and emergency procedures across two
     training days. Proper evaluation and assessment of crew performance is key to
     improving individual and crew skills prior to moving to the live-fire events.

     3-74. Units can maximize their AARs and assessments of training by fully integrating
     CVTESS, HITS, LTIDs, and CMU/IPU/CMUR systems in the training event. These
     systems used together provide a near-digital range facility capability at the company and
     battalion level.

FIRING GROUP AFTER ACTION REVIEWS–TABLE III

     3-75. For the firing group, each crew is given an AAR at the completion of the day phase
     and night phase by a qualified VCE. Use DA Form 8265-1 to record and track the results
     of each crew’s performance on each engagement at the completion of each phase.
     Tracking crew and platoon statistics on each engagement allow the unit leadership to
     identify areas for improvement.

             Note. VCEs must be certified or recertified as described in TC 3-20.31.
             Performing the duties as a VCE during the conduct of Table III, STX is a
             significant part of building experience and knowledge of those gunners and
             VCs without firing.

    3-76. Units should track the various firing information from the score sheets to  identify
    where in the conduct of fire crews and the overall unit should apply additional   training
    effort. Some of those indicators are—

         • Target acquisition, detection, and identification.
         • Time to fire.
         • Time to kill.
         • Opening and closing times for machine gun engagements.
         • Gunner lay errors.
         • Scanning techniques.
         • Engagement techniques.
         • Conduct of fire issues.
         • Penalties by type.
         • Missile tracking technique.

    3-77. Each crew should receive a formal AAR following the guidelines found in
    TC 3-20.31. Each evaluator should use DA Form 8265 and DA Form 8265-1 as the basis
    for their evaluation, identifying the firing occasional information from the score sheet
    timelines. The evaluator and VC each review and sign the DA Form 8265-1.

94         TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                     Table III–Situational Training Exercise

CRITICAL SKILLS TASK AFTER ACTION REVIEWS–TABLE III

     3-78. OC/Ts should provide clear and articulate comments and insight about the positive
     and negative actions of the crews they observed. OC/Ts should use the T&EOs to guide
     them through the evaluation process and use the doctrinal publications to support their
     comments.

     3-79. The OC/T and unit leadership should review the T&EOs and training notes and
     observations to determine which tasks require additional attention through the remainder
     of the training density.

     3-80. The OC/T should share the overall performance of the unit, including the
     observations, with the entire training unit. This allows the subordinate units to learn from
     other unit’s best practices, procedures, and tactics, and at times from their shortcomings
     or mistakes. The commander should identify crews within the organization that
     demonstrate superior performance.

     3-81. At the completion of this portion of crew Table III, STX, commanders, executive
     officers, and 1SGs should gain insight to where their unit’s SOPs are working and where
     they need additional attention. If time is available, commanders should update and
     provide those changes or revisions to the unit prior to executing any live-fire event. Of
     particular interest is the drivers’ training program, vehicle load plans, stowage of
     individual tactical gear, crew evacuation procedures, and medical evacuation
     procedures.

RETRAIN–TABLE III

     3-82. The unit Master Gunner maintains the unit roll-up by engagement. A
     subcategorized roll-up by platoons provides the commander a snapshot of training
     trends, strengths, and weaknesses. The Master Gunner should not only identify crews
     that did not achieve a passing standard, but also crews that struggled on specific
     engagement tasks. Once done, the unit can establish and conduct the appropriate focused
     retraining followed by a subsequent evaluation.

     3-83. All crews must successfully complete the course of fire based on the standards for
     the engagements listed earlier in this chapter. No crew may proceed to Table IV or any
     other live-fire event, including zeroing, or sub-caliber events until they have achieved
     the minimum standard on Table III.

     3-84. Platoon sergeants and platoon leaders should identify crews that require additional
     training on the various crew critical skills. Although the evaluation may be completed,
     units should integrate sustainment training into their unit training plans, particularly
     when environmental conditions change, movement during limited visibility is required,
     or planning or expecting tactical movement through restricted or rugged terrain.

                                  Chapter 4

                    Table IV–Basic

Table IV, Basic, is designed to train basic skills at a reduced tempo to enable
proper leader and Soldier execution of those basic crew skills. This table
builds upon those skills trained and tested during Tables I, II, and III. It is a
combination of primary armament zero and machine gun zero. For select
crew platform types with a commander’s weapon station or independent
viewer (commander’s independent thermal viewer, for example) a course of
fire using full-caliber training ammunition on an authorized live-fire facility,
as described in the respective crew platform’s appendix.

The design of Table IV is to train and evaluate the crew’s ability to engage
single and multiple stationary or moving targets placed in a tactical array,
during the day or night, with an operational or simulated degraded fire
control system, as applicable. Crews execute these tasks from the defense
using full-caliber ammunition for zero and tactical engagements.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  103


---

Chapter 4

TABLE IV OVERVIEW

     4-1. Table IV provides crews the ability to align their fire control system, screen or zero
     their ammunition by type, and complete basic main gun and machine gun engagement
     skills from a defensive firing position. There are two task sets to Table IV, Basic:

          • Zeroing.
          • Defensive engagements.

     4-2. Completion of zeroing procedures must occur successfully before Table IV, Basic.
     This is to ensure the fire control system aligns correctly, and the ammunition can hit the
     target as expected ballistically. The defensive engagements are day or night
     engagements depending on the unit’s plan.

     4-3. Figure 4-1 shows where Table IV, Basic, resides within the BFV training strategy
     through crew qualification. This is the first event with the crew operating the platform
     under live-fire conditions. This course of fire includes zeroing as part of the training
     event (see figure 4-1).

                                     Figure 4-1. Table IV, Basic

             Note. Table IV, Practice, may be executed day or night. Engagement split is
             determined by the unit based on time and resources available.

TABLE IV PURPOSE

     4-4. Table IV, Basic, is a skills assessment and risk mitigation tool for the commander.
     It assists the commander in determining the safe and effective use of the platform, its
     weapons, identification of threats and common ammunition by the crewmembers in the
     unit. Further, it identifies shortfalls and training gaps in those gunnery specific skills,
     which build crew proficiency and unit readiness.

104  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                                      Table IV–Basic

TABLE IV METHOD

     4-5. Table IV is a hands-on, performance-based evaluation of basic gunnery skills to the
     Army-standard of knowledge or skill in a live-fire environment. It utilizes experienced
     evaluators to educate, train, test, and retrain Soldiers to meet or exceed those standards.

TABLE IV END STATE

     4-6. The VC/gunner assigned as the crew have successfully demonstrated the skills and
     knowledge required to continue training on the platform through platform qualification.
     Commanders are confident in their Soldier’s abilities in the position assigned.

     4-7. The tasks evaluated in Table IV provide the commander a means to verify the
     crew’s basic weapons platform proficiency prior to further live-fire training. It is a guide
     for the commander to identify individual and crew weapons platform strengths and
     weaknesses on specific engagement techniques and employment of the vehicle’s fire
     control system capabilities and enablers. The commander and Master Gunner use the
     results from Table IV, Basic to structure the unit’s crew training program.

TABLE IV TASK, CONDITIONS, AND STANDARD

     4-8. The two portions of Table IV, Basic, have their own unique set of task, conditions,
     and standards. Task presentation is sequential for clarity.

Zeroing Optics and Fire Control System

     4-9. This portion of Table IV is to ensure the vehicle and crew can accurately align the
     platform’s optics, and that the platform’s fire control system is calculating an accurate
     ballistic solution for primary armament and coaxial machine gun engagements. Further,
     this ensures each sub-system of the fire control system is functioning properly after all
     non-firing preparation to fire checks are complete. (The action, conditions, and standards
     for Table IV, Basic, zeroing is provided in figure 4-2 on page 106.)

                   Army Standard Zero Distance, 25mm

          The Army standard for zero of the 25-mm, M242 chain gun has
          been changed to 600 meters, utilizing the standard target
          number 7 panel. This provides the crew a target panel with the
          correct dimensions based on round dispersion and reference lines
          to allow precise reticle alignment between the target acquisition
          system (TAS)/direct view optic (DVO) with the gun reticle selected,
          and AUX sights.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  105


---

Chapter 4

      Figure 4-2. Table IV, Basic, Zeroing–task, conditions, and standards

             Note. Due to the requirement of using the DVO if equipped and AUX sight
             alignment, zeroing must be conducted during daylight conditions.

Defensive Engagement Requirement

     4-10. Completion of zeroing procedure must occur successfully before conducting
     Table IV, Basic. Vehicles that fail to zero may not progress to any live-fire table
     including the defensive engagements. (The task, conditions, and standard for Table IV,
     Basic, defensive engagements, are in figure 4-3.)

106        TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025

                             Table IV–Basic

       Figure 4-3. Table IV, Basic, task, conditions, and standards

       Note. If any individual target score on an engagement is less than 70 points,
       the engagement is unqualified, regardless of overall engagement score.

4-11. Crews should perform Table IV, Basic, defensive engagements, on the crew’s
vehicle. It is designed to train and evaluate the crew’s ability to engage single and
multiple stationary and moving targets placed in a tactical array, during the day or night,
with a fully operational or simulated degraded fire control system, as depicted in the
task, conditions, and standard for each engagement. Crews execute these tasks from the
defense once zeroing is complete.

4-12. Crews may execute this table using TADSS only when ammunition is unavailable
or to conduct retraining after live-fire training is complete. Units should use 3/4-scale
targets at full range for all scenarios but may consider using full-scale based on the unit’s
level of expertise. The Master Gunner may consider using F-type silhouettes rather than
E-type silhouettes within the rocket propelled grenade and troop target sets.
(See TC 25-8 for more information.)

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  107


---

Chapter 4

PLAN FOR TABLE IV

     4-13. Table IV, Basic, is a live environment, live-fire event that uses large- and
     small-caliber ammunition. It will require the use of appended training aids, devices, and
     simulators, and the use of BESs during the defensive engagements.

     4-14. Table IV is a live-fire event that consists of two task sets: zeroing and defensive
     engagements. Each set requires particular attention during planning to ensure the unit
     has the appropriate support on the range(s).

     4-15. This section details the resources needed for planning purposes. It includes general
     information on the following topics for consideration:

          • Uniform—the minimum uniform requirements for the training event.
          • Facilities—available facilities that support the training event.
          • Standard targets—specifically used for zeroing procedures for the main gun

                and machine guns, these targets are required to conduct Table IV.
          • Training plan—the minimum tasks, general guidelines, and special instructions

                for the training event.
          • Time and throughput—the estimated time required to complete the training

                event including throughput considerations for planning.
          • Ammunition—any training munitions required to complete the training event.

                This includes DDI items that require coordination.
          • TADSS—any available training aids that directly support or augment the

                training event.

     4-16. Table IV is a critical live-fire event. Failing to conduct the zeroing procedures for
     the platform accurately will cripple any follow-on live-fire event outcomes. Units can
     separate the zeroing procedures for the main gun and small arms from the defensive
     engagements to maximize throughput.

     4-17. Units may require coordination with installation support activities such as range
     operations for specific targetry requirements for zeroing. Each installation’s policies and
     procedures vary. Units should reference their supporting installation’s training SOPs or
     policies for detailed information.

108  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                                      Table IV–Basic

TABLE IV UNIFORM

     4-18. Crews must be in the appropriate vehicle crewmember uniform or prescribed unit
     uniform and protective mask. Crews are not required to have any additional body armor,
     ballistic plates, or gear during the training event unless directed by the commander.
     (See figure 4-4 for the minimum uniform training requirements for this event.)

TABLE IV FACILITIES

     4-19. Table IV requires the use of a stationary gunnery range or better that allows live
     fire for small- and medium-caliber ammunition. No course road is necessary; however,
     moving armor targets are required. All tasks are from a stationary firing position. To
     maximize throughput, units may elect to use multiple range facilities; one for zeroing
     procedures, and another for the defensive engagements that require single and multiple
     stationary and moving targetry. (See figure 4-5 for the maneuver range facilities
     recommended as primary and alternate facilities.)

TABLE IV RANGE SUPPORT

     4-20. The range support group provides VCE teams to evaluate the course of fire. This
     requires all gunners and above to certify or recertify as VCEs between T-6 and
     T-execution of the training event.
     4-21. The range support personnel general requirements include the most common
     support required for crew platform live-fire events (see figure 4-6). In the event the unit
     utilizes multiple facilities to complete the zero and defensive engagements, appropriate
     adjustments should be made to this general structure.

Chapter 4

TABLE IV TRAINING PLAN

     4-22. Table IV is unique within the training strategy in that it completes two separate
     requirements for the crews on a live-fire facility or facilities. The training tasks are—

          • Zeroing.
          • Defensive engagements.
     4-23. Some installations will offer or require the use of two separate facilities to
     complete Table IV. One specific to zeroing and another facility for the stationary
     defensive engagements. Having multiple ranges is helpful to ensure sufficient time is
     available for those vehicles having difficulty zeroing their weapons. (An example
     conduct of the range flow is in figure 4-7.) Units should consider requesting additional
     VCE support to maximize range time and prep-to-fire checks and boresighting
     procedures before first round down range.

                Figure 4-7. Table IV conduct of the range flow example

Zero Procedures

     4-24. Conduct of the zero is on a live-fire facility that can support primary armament as
     well as small arms surface danger zones. Crews will follow the procedures outlined in
     this manual.
     4-25. Once complete with the zeroing process for the 25-mm M242 chain gun, the crew
     then confirms zero with the coaxial machine gun. Crews follow the procedures outlined
     in this chapter (See figures 4-14 on page 125 through figure 4-27 on page 138.)

     4-27. Once complete with zeroing for the 25-mm cannon, the crew confirms the zero for

Table IV Scenario Requirements

     4-28. This table is designed to train and evaluate the crew’s ability to engage stationary
     or moving targets placed in a tactical array, from the defense, during day or limited
     visibility conditions.

     4-29. All engagements are evaluated using certified VCE’s using DA Form 8265 and
     DA Form 8265-1 as the official record of training. Master Gunners must ensure the
     training records are included in each crew’s gunnery packet (see TC 3-20.31).

     4-30. The engagements listings are by capability type using the standard engagement
     naming structure listed in TC 3-20.31. It provides the requirements for each engagement,
     described in paragraphs 4-31 through 4-38.

114  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                                      Table IV–Basic

Task Description–Table IV
     4-31. The task description includes both the engagement number (the table number plus
     engagement task) followed by the system capability trained. The use of a letter, for
     example, 41(A) denotes an additional engagement for that task.

Firing Vehicle Posture–Table IV
     4-32. The firing vehicle posture establishes the offense or defense condition for the
     engagement.

Firer–Table IV
     4-33. The firer column identifies which crewmember is to engage the target listed.
     Targets defeated by a different crewmember listed as the firer result in an auto-zero
     penalty, failing to adhere to course conditions.

Weapon (or Ammunition)–Table IV
     4-34. Identifies the weapon (coaxial, or primary armament ammunition type) that is used
     to defeat the threat. Design of each engagement is for a specific weapon or ammunition
     type (primary armament) for use, which relates to the crew conduct of fire, optic
     selection, engagement techniques, or other unique implied tasks. Crews that fail to
     announce the appropriate weapon or ammunition, or appropriate selection for
     subsequent or supplemental engagements may be penalized IAW TC 3-20.31.

Target Type–Table IV
     4-35. The target type is required for the presentation, IAW TC 25-8.

Target Posture–Table IV
     4-36. The target posture establishes if the target is stationary, moving, or evasive for the
     engagement. See TC 3-20.31 for more information on target postures.

Range (to Target)–Table IV
     4-37. The required range (full scale or equivalent scaled range) from the firing vehicle
     to the target. For offensive engagements, this distance calculation is from the center of
     the movement box to the target. The range to target distance is provided in meters (for
     example, 600). The target may be placed at the distance indicated, plus or minus
     200 meters. For example, if the range to target listed is 600 meters, the target may be
     placed between 400 and 800 meters. No change to the EM is authorized.

Engagement Modifier–Table IV
     4-38. The EM is the time in seconds required to achieve 100 points against the listed
     threat. To defeat the threat successfully, the crew must achieve the kill standard against
     the target and have an overall engagement time of no more than the EM plus 20 seconds.
     See TC 3-20.31 for the evaluation, timing, and scoring procedures.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  115

Chapter 4

TRAINING AIDS, DEVICES, SIMULATORS, AND SIMULATIONS–TABLE IV

     4-42. This course requires the use of the CMU (some installations may refer to this
     device as an IPU) for all crews. The CMU is available on the digital range facility
     scheduled (if equipped). Coordination with the scheduled range facility manager will
     determine unit responsibilities for installation, troubleshooting, maintenance, and use of
     the devices ahead of the training day(s). If the range does not have a CMU available,
     unit may draw the CMUR from the TADSS warehouse. The CMUR differs from the
     CMU in that it stores all run data on a solid-state drive on the vehicle, for use with the
     provided AAR suite in the CMUR kit.

     4-43. The brigade commander must approve authorization for deviation from this
     requirement if TADSS are unavailable in writing. Units should focus on training and
     reinforcing the skills in the direct fire kill chain on this table specifically with their
     thermal optics, day and night to best prepare them for the follow-on training events.
     (See figure 4-13 for the TADSS required or recommended for Table IV.)

    Figure 4-13. Table IV training aids, devices, simulators, and simulations
                                              requirements

PREPARE FOR TABLE IV

     4-44. Units must coordinate for the appropriate training facility, equipment, targetry,
     training munitions, TADSS (if desired), and pyrotechnics to support Table IV. During
     the preparation phase, the unit should ensure the proofing team, VCE’s, and other
     personnel receive training to standard to support their role as evaluators and trainers.
     This includes range officers in charge, range safety officers, and ammunition handlers.

TRAIN THE TRAINERS–TABLE IV

     4-45. Typically, units have certified each VC and most gunners as VCEs prior to
     entering the live-fire density. Units must have certified evaluators to ensure the training
     objectives for the training table are set and to provide effective AARs to each crew.
     Table IV also requires experienced VCs to serve as the proofing team for the zero.

120  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                                      Table IV–Basic

Vehicle Crew Evaluators–Table IV

     4-46. A trained and certified VCE team must be available to support prior to live fire to
     evaluate a gunnery density correctly. Once certification is completed, the unit must
     ensure sufficient VCEs are available to support the live-fire event. Evaluations of crew
     gunnery always come from outside the firing platoon element, and for qualification
     purposes, VCEs external to the battalion are required.

     4-47. Depending on the size of the weapons training density (make-up gunnery, platoon,
     company, or battalion) and the type of range facility used, the number of evaluators may
     vary. Having the correct number and type of evaluators per firing range maximizes the
     unit’s throughput of firing vehicles on the range, reduce range down time, and provide
     for the highest quality evaluations.

Proofing Team

     4-48. The unit Master Gunner provides training to the proofing team’s members on the
     following topics at a minimum:

          • Preparation to fire checks.
          • Special gunnery checks.
          • M1 gunner’s quadrant.
          • Zero procedures.
          • Determination of mean point of impact.
          • DA Form 2408-4 (Weapon Record Data).
          • Fire control system overview.

     4-49. These overview classes are required to ensure the Master Gunner has sufficient
     support during the zero, particularly with conduct of the range and procedures.

     4-50. Units provide the following to prepare the trainers:
          • Commander’s training objectives.
          • Conduct of the range briefing for the training event.
          • Scenario and lane concept with crew tasks for training.
          • Risk assessment.
          • TADSS, pyrotechnics, blanks, and BESs used during the training event (see
                appendix A for the training munitions requirements for the complete training
                strategy).

RECONNAISSANCE OF THE SITE–TABLE IV

     4-51. Commanders use an authorized training facility for Table IV, Basic. Units may
     elect to use two separate training facilities to support all the Table IV tasks efficiently.
     Commanders follow the installation’s policies and procedures for formally scheduling
     the training location as far in advance as practical. See TC 3-20.31 for more in-depth
     information concerning range operations at the unit level.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  121


---

Chapter 4

     4-52. Once scheduled, the commander, Master Gunner, platoon leaders, and platoon
     sergeants conduct a reconnaissance of the training areas to—

          • Verify sufficient space is available to support the entire unit through multiple
                training lanes.

          • Ensure multiple iterations or rotations through the scenario are achievable with
                appropriate retraining opportunities.

          • Ensure sufficient administrative areas are available to support group
                discussions, briefings, and AARs.

          • Provide sand table or terrain board.
          • Identify the ASP.
          • Determine the conduct of the range or the flow of training to maximize the

                live-fire range time.
          • Identify the range operating system and verify the training scenarios have been

                properly loaded.
          • Schedule proofing of the range, based on the higher headquarters OPORD.

ISSUE THE ORDER–TABLE IV

     4-53. Commanders can issue an OPORD, WARNORD, or use the training schedule as
     necessary. The WARNORD should include the appropriate tasks, required leaders, and
     equipment needed, as well as the unit’s training timeline including walk-through,
     rehearsals, and AARs. WARNORDs for external organizations must include sufficient
     reaction time for the appropriate personnel and equipment needed.

REHEARSE–TABLE IV

     4-54. The unit uses all training resources, materials, and equipment available to conduct
     rehearsals of training events. During the rehearsal, commanders ensure their unit has
     sufficient time to train to the specified standard including AARs and retraining time.
     Commanders ensure their unit verifies optional scenarios that increase complexity when
     warranted.

     4-55. Units should rehearse the training event from start to finish, and confirm the
     following:

          • The VCEs and proofing team understand the training scenarios and tasks.
          • Sufficient space is available to provide appropriate distances for engagements

                ranges and target dispersion.
          • Analog and digital systems are operational within the organization and to the

                higher headquarters regardless of their location.
          • Additional crew training packages (DA Form 8265, DA Form 8265-1, and

                appropriate zero documentation) are complete and provide for multiple
                iterations.
          • Sufficient administrative areas are available.

122  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                             Table IV–Basic

          • Review of AAR capabilities and use of thru-sight video systems is encouraged
                to increase the fidelity and quality of the AARs.

          • Location of retraining is established for crews as necessary.
          • Accessibility to crew simulator systems, plumb and synchronization ramps,

                tracking and manipulation panels, and additional boresighting locations are
                available.

     4-56. Once the unit completes the walk-through and training rehearsal, the commander
     makes any necessary adjustments to the plan and issues FRAGORDs as necessary.

EXECUTE TABLE IV

     4-57. Once the unit arrives at the training location, they should—
          • Conduct a safety briefing for the training event.
          • Provide a conduct of the range briefing to establish the sequence of events for
                the training day.
          • Conduct sensitive item checks prior to executing training.
          • Assign OC/Ts as appropriate.
          • Issue the OPORD for the training mission.
          • Execute the training scenario.
          • Install any TADSS as required.

     4-58. The training unit conducts informal AARs of each crew completing a phase of
     training within the training mission. During training, the assigned OC/T, executive
     officer, 1SG, and Master Gunner review the subordinate unit’s performance. They
     follow the unit SOP, prescribed TTP, and the appropriate T&EOs, and review completed
     crew score sheets and roll-up documentation for clarity and completeness.

ZEROING–EXECUTION OF TABLE IV

                 Meteorological Parameters

Prior to any boresighting or zeroing procedures crews should
ensure their meteorological parameters have been updated based
on current weather conditions. Reference applicable vehicle variant
technical manual for specific information regarding these
procedures.

4-59. Once crews have completed their prep-to-fire checks, boresighting, and other
alignment procedures, they conduct the zero procedures. The Master Gunner is
responsible for controlling the firing order, vehicle movement, and procedures for
zeroing from the vehicle technical manual and this chapter.

4-60. The proofing team will check any crew that fails the zero process. The unit Master
Gunner and maintenance chief will determine the most probable cause for the zero
failure and take appropriate measures to correct the deficiencies, whether mechanical,
ammunition specific, or crew induced.

4-61. Once the crew is complete with zero for all primary armament ammunition types,
the crew completes coaxial machine gun zero. Typically, the VC/gunner is responsible

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25                                          123

     for the conduct of the machine gun zeroing process after receiving clearance to fire from
     the tower.

     4-62. As the crew completes the zeroing process and is prepared to conduct the
     engagements phase, based on the conduct of the range, the crew will move to the
     designated holding area (firing order) and await instructions to proceed.

     4-63. Zeroing the 25-mm is conducted using M910, target-practice, discarding sabot
     with tracer ammunition, more commonly known as TPDS-T ammunition, in single shot
     mode, using a standard target number 7 panel at 600 meters (see figure 4-14 for an
     example of standard target number 7, 25-mm zero). This provides the crew with a point
     of reference close enough to clearly identify the point of round impacts and provides
     accurate references to precisely align the TAS gun reticle, through the DVO if equipped,
     and AUX sight reticles.

     4-66. Once the commander has entered the zero procedures in the commander’s tactical
     display, and the following conditions are met, the crew performs the following steps to
     complete the zero process (see figure 4-17 on page 128 to figure 4-27 on page 138):

          • Null the system.
          • Align TAS if required by the improved Bradley acquisition system message.
          • Open sight shield doors.
          • Place turret drive system in manual mode (gun elevation and traverse in

                manual, TOW elevation in POWER).
          • Perform the following functional checks on the 25-mm gun system:

                 Ensuring manual sear retractor is disengaged.
                 Ensuring the straight drive shaft to the feeder is locked in place.
                 Ensuring the feed and eject chutes are connected.
                 Dry cycling the 25-mm gun twice in HE (high explosive) and twice in AP

                     (armor piercing [in power mode]).
          • Identify zero target panel through the TAS/DVO and correct range to target is

                indexed with the laser range finder or manually indexed using the gunners sight
                control panel (range to target should be 600 meters plus or minus 10 meters).

DEFENSIVE ENGAGEMENTS

     4-68. As the tower is prepared to conduct the defensive engagements, they will control
     the movement of all vehicles in the firing order. The tower will direct the crew to the
     appropriate defensive firing position and await further instructions.

     4-69. Once set, the tower directs the crew to test fire their machine guns, move to a red
     status, and report when set. As the crew completes the test firing, they report set to the
     tower. As the conduct of the range allows, the tower directs the crew to conduct the
     engagements for evaluation. Units must enforce the appropriate firing position within
     the battle positions to reinforce their proper tactical use (see figure 4-28).

Chapter 4

ASSESS –TABLE IV

     4-80. The intent of the basic course of fire is to train the crews within the organization
     on the appropriate conduct of fire skills in a defensive posture against common threat
     arrays using their primary and secondary armament. Proper evaluation and assessment
     of crew performance is key to improving individual and crew skills prior to moving to
     the practice and qualification course of fire. This includes conducting AARs for the
     zeroing and the live-fire engagements.

ZEROING ASSESSMENT–TABLE IV

     4-81. For Table IV, the zeroing portion of the event is a maintenance and crew
     confidence exercise. The crew must ensure the fire control system is functioning as
     intended after all prep-to-fire procedures have been complete. In doing so, it provides
     the crew confidence in the procedures, policies, equipment, ammunition, and their own
     skill. The platoon sergeant or Master Gunner should provide an informal AAR for
     zeroing procedures that includes at a minimum:

          • Actions for each ammunition type.
          • Location of hits for each ammunition type.
          • Review of fixed biases, variable biases, and random errors that contribute to

                the accuracy of the round.
          • Zeroing procedures for each machine gun.
          • Methods to reduce round-to-round dispersion and crew error.

DEFENSIVE ENGAGEMENT ASSESSMENT–TABLE IV

     4-82. Each crew should receive a formal AAR following the guidelines found in
     TC 3-20.31. Each evaluator should use DA Form 8265 and DA Form 8265-1 as the basis
     for their evaluation, identifying the firing occasional information from the score sheet
     timelines. The evaluator and VC each review and sign the DA Form 8265-1.

             Note. Performing the duties as a VCE during the conduct of Table IV, Basic,
             is a significant part of building experience and knowledge of those gunners
             and VCs without firing.

     4-83. Units should track the various firing information from the score sheets to  identify
     where in the conduct of fire crews and the overall unit should apply additional   training
     effort. Some of those indicators are—

          • Target acquisition, detection, and identification.
          • Time to fire.
          • Time to kill.
          • Opening and closing times for machine gun engagements.
          • Gunner lay errors.
          • Scanning techniques.
          • Engagement techniques.
          • Conduct of fire issues.
          • Penalties by type.

160        TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025

                                                                                                      Table IV–Basic

RETRAIN–TABLE IV

     4-84. The unit Master Gunner maintains the unit roll-up by engagement. A
     subcategorized roll-up by platoons provides the commander a snapshot of training
     trends, strengths, and weaknesses. The Master Gunner should not only identify crews
     that did not achieve a passing standard, but also crews that struggled on specific
     engagement tasks. Once done, the unit can establish and conduct the appropriate focused
     retraining followed by a subsequent evaluation.

             Note. All crews should successfully complete the course of fire based on the
             standards for the engagements listed earlier in this chapter. No crew should
             proceed to Table V or any other live-fire event until they have achieved the
             minimum standard on Table IV. The commander and Master Gunner may
             assess the crews’ capability to proceed without qualifying the table based on
             target acquisition, target kills, fire commands, and engagement techniques
             without achieving the minimum standard.

     4-85. Unit Master Gunners should include chair drills for the crews using the scenarios
     for each engagement for retraining purposes. Consider including additional low-impact
     training drills to meet the demands of the 3 and 4 target engagements. Units should
     include future table’s engagements (Table V and Table VI, respectively) in the chair
     drills training to best prepare the crews for those events. Additional information on chair
     drills is in TC 3-20.31.

DRIVER’S TRAINING–TABLE IV

     4-86. Leaders should determine the number of miles driven during the day and night
     phases for their assigned drivers. This information should be recorded, including the
     miles driven, miles driven with the enhanced viewer, and any tactical (off-road) miles
     driven.

CREW GUNNERY FOLDER RECORDS–TABLE IV

     4-87. Once testing is complete, the Master Gunner records the results of all training
     completed in the crew gunnery folder. The records must be maintained for a minimum
     of 12 months.

RECORD INDIVIDUAL TASK PERFORMANCE–TABLE IV

     4-88. Leaders track and train their Soldier’s individual and collective tasks trained and
     evaluated using tools provided by the ATN such as the SULT and the DJB. These
     systems interface directly with the DTMS to record training and evaluation results.

             Note. Leaders must be granted access first by their commander or DTMS
             manager to enroll or assign their Soldiers the specific tasks from the ICTL
             that are trained and tested during the training event.

     4-91. This ensures historical data is available throughout the Army force generation

                                      Chapter 5

                      Table V–Practice

    Crew platform, Table V, Practice, is a live-environment training event under
    live-fire conditions using full-caliber training ammunition, 3/4-scale
    realistic targetry and engagement scenarios, at a combat tempo on an
    authorized live-fire facility. Regardless of crew platform type, this table
    reinforces the basic skills trained during previous events but increases the
    tempo to expected engagement sequence capabilities to build leader and
    element tactical and technical mastery.
    This table includes execution of critical skills in a live environment. Table V
    prepares the crew to employ their weapons and systems against most
    common threat scenarios using prescribed targetry to a required standard.

             Note. This table requires the use of 3/4-scale targets to reinforce crew
             engagement speed and precision prior to qualification.

TABLE V OVERVIEW

     5-1. Table V, Practice, trains and evaluate the crew’s ability to engage stationary or
     moving targets placed in a tactical array, during day and limited visibility conditions,
     with an operational or simulated degraded fire control system, as applicable. Crews
     execute these tasks from the offense and defense using full-caliber ammunition against
     3/4-scale targets placed at full range.

     5-2. This table requires each crew to successfully complete the live-fire prerequisites,
     all zeroing procedures for their platform, and complete the defensive engagements on
     Table IV. This table is designed to train complex engagement skills, day and night, while
     fighting from the defense and during offensive engagements.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  169


---

Chapter 5

     5-3. Table V, Practice, resides within the BFV training strategy through crew
     qualification (see figure 5-1). Table V is the first live-fire event where the crew conducts
     live-fire main gun engagements from the defense, as well as complex engagements
     while on the offense. It is a core training event for the crews that provides experience
     across multifaceted tactical dilemmas at a battlefield tempo.

     Figure 5-1. Table V, Practice

170  TC 3-20.31-25                  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                                   Table V–Practice

TABLE V PURPOSE

     5-4. Table V is a skills assessment and risk mitigation tool for the commander. It assists
     the commander in determining the safe and effective use of the platform, its weapons,
     identification of threats and common ammunition by the crewmembers in the unit.
     Further, it identifies shortfalls and training gaps in those gunnery specific skills, which
     build crew proficiency and unit readiness.

TABLE V METHOD

     5-5. Table V is a performance-based evaluation of basic and advanced gunnery skills to
     the Army-standard of knowledge or skill in a live environment under live-fire
     conditions. Conduct of Table V is on vehicles using full-caliber ammunition, BESs, and
     3/4-scale targetry at full-distance to provide the most challenging training prior to the
     qualification course of fire. It utilizes experienced evaluators to educate, train, test, and
     retrain Soldiers to meet or exceed those standards.

TABLE V END STATE

     5-6. The VC and gunner assigned as a crew have successfully demonstrated the skills
     and knowledge required to continue training on the platform through platform
     qualification. Commanders are confident in their Soldier’s abilities in the position
     assigned. The tasks evaluated in Table V provide the commander a means to certify the
     crew’s basic and advanced weapons platform proficiency prior to the qualification
     course of fire using full-caliber munitions. The commander and Master Gunner use the
     results from the qualification course when structuring the unit’s crew training program
     and is a readiness reportable item.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  171


---

Chapter 5

TABLE V TASK, CONDITIONS, AND STANDARD

     5-7. Units should not add to the live-fire requirements or tasks associated with Table V.
     If the commander is confident with the crew’s skills, they may increase the CBRN
     requirements from one day and one night engagement to suit their experience.
     (See figure 5-2 for the task, conditions, and standard for Table V, Practice.)

           Figure 5-2. Table V, Practice, task, conditions, and standards
             Note. If any individual target score on an engagement is less than 70 points,
             the engagement is unqualified, regardless of overall engagement score.

172  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025

                                                                                                   Table V–Practice

PLAN FOR TABLE V

     5-8. Table V, Practice, is a live-fire training environment event using training aids,
     devices, and simulators, and includes the use of full-caliber and small arms training
     ammunition and BESs.

     5-9. This section details the resources needed for planning purposes. It includes general
     information on the following topics for consideration:

          • Uniform—the minimum requirements for the combat vehicle crewmember’s
                fire-retardant clothing and protective equipment.

          • Facilities—available facilities that support the training event.
          • Training plan—the minimum tasks, general guidelines, and special instructions

                for the training event.
          • Time and throughput—the estimated time required to complete the training

                event including throughput considerations for planning.
          • Ammunition—any training munitions required to complete the training event.
          • TADSS—any available training aids that directly support or augment the

                training event.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  173

Chapter 5

TABLE V UNIFORM

     5-10. Crews must be in the appropriate vehicle crewmember uniform or prescribed unit
     uniform and protective mask. Crews are not required to have any additional body armor,
     ballistic plates, or gear during the training event unless directed by the commander.
     (See figure 5-3 for the minimum uniform training requirements this event.)

     Figure 5-3. Table V uniform requirements

TABLE V FACILITIES

     5-11. Table V requires the use of a maneuver gunnery range that allows live fire for
     medium caliber and 7.62-mm small-caliber ammunition. To maximize throughput, units
     may elect to use multiple lanes on the facility.
     5-12. Master Gunners must be familiar with the family of maneuver ranges, the range
     operations control area, and the specific instrumentation and target sets available on the
     range facility selected for Table V. (See figure 5-4 for the maneuver range facilities
     recommended as primary and alternate facilities.)

              Figure 5-4. Table V capable maneuver range facilities

Chapter 5

TABLE V RANGE SUPPORT

     5-13. The range support group provides VCE teams to evaluate the course of fire. This
     requires all gunners and above to certify or recertify as VCEs between T-6 and
     T-execution of the training event.
     5-14. This includes the most common support required for crew platform live-fire
     events. In the event the unit utilizes multiple facilities to complete the zero and defensive
     engagements, appropriate adjustments should be made to this general structure.
     (See figure 5-5 for the range support personnel general requirements.)

     Figure 5-5. Range Support personnel requirements, example

176  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                                   Table V–Practice

TABLE V TRAINING PLAN

     5-15. Table V, Practice, provides a challenging scenario to the crews to prepare them
     for qualification and future collective training. It relies on engagements with a
     moderately higher level of difficulty that the qualification course of fire that exercises
     the crew’s conduct of fire and engagement skills. The increased difficulty in relation to
     the qualification course of fire is provided through—

          • Smaller targetry.
          • Increased number of targets.
          • Increased range to target for the majority of the engagements.
          • Greater dispersion between targets laterally and in-depth.

     5-16. Units are required to use 3/4-scale targets at full range for the training scenarios
     unless specifically directed to use full-scale targetry (degraded engagements, for
     example). The Master Gunner has the option to use F-type rather than E-type silhouettes
     to increase the difficulty during machine gun engagements. (See TC 25-8 for additional
     information on scaled targetry.)

     5-17. This table prepares crews for the Table VI, Qualification. The table design is
     purposefully more difficult to master than the qualification. Units should retrain as
     necessary with full-caliber ammunition when possible. Units should use available
     resources to ensure all crews successfully complete Table V before proceeding to the
     qualification course of fire, Table VI.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  177


---

Chapter 5

Table V Scenario Requirements

     5-18. This table is designed to train and evaluate the crew’s ability to engage stationary
     or moving targets placed in a tactical array, from the defense, during day and limited
     visibility conditions.

     5-19. Units’ must use 3/4-scale targets at full range for all scenarios except degraded
     engagements. Units may consider using full-scale for degraded engagements based on
     the units’ level of expertise. The Master Gunner may consider using F-type silhouettes
     rather than E-type silhouettes. (See TC 25-8 for more information.)

     5-20. All engagements are evaluated using certified VCE’s using DA Form 8265 and
     DA Form 8265-1 as the official record of training. Master Gunners must ensure the
     training records are included in each crew’s gunnery packet (see TC 3-20.31).

     5-21. The engagements listings are by capability type using the standard engagement
     naming structure listed in TC 3-20.31. It provides the requirements for each engagement,
     which are described in paragraphs 5-22 through 5-29.

Task Description–Table V

     5-22. The task description includes both the engagement number (the table number plus
     engagement task) followed by the system capability trained.

Firing Vehicle Posture–Table V

     5-23. The firing vehicle posture establishes the offense or defense condition for the
     engagement.

Firer–Table V

     5-24. The firer column identifies which crewmember is to engage the target listed.
     Targets defeated by a different crewmember listed as the firer result in an auto-zero
     penalty, failing to adhere to course conditions.

Weapon (or Ammunition)–Table V

     5-25. The weapon or ammunition column identifies the weapon (coaxial, or primary
     armament ammunition type) that is used to defeat the threat. Design of each engagement
     is for a specific weapon or ammunition type (primary armament) for use, which relates
     to the crew conduct of fire, optic selection, engagement techniques, or other unique
     implied tasks. Crews that fail to announce the appropriate weapon or ammunition, or
     appropriate selection for subsequent or supplemental engagements may be penalized
     IAW evaluation procedures.

Target Type

     5-26. The target type is the target required for the presentation, IAW TC 25-8.

Target Posture–Table V

     5-27. The target posture establishes if the target is stationary, moving, or evasive for the
     engagement. See TC 3-20.31 for more information on target postures.

178        TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                                   Table V–Practice

Range (to Target)–Table V

     5-28. The range column is the required range (full scale or equivalent scaled range) from
     the firing vehicle to the target. For offensive engagements, this distance calculation is
     from the center of the movement box to the target. The range to target distance is
     provided in meters (for example, 600). The target may be placed at the distance
     indicated, plus or minus 200-meters. For example, the target may be placed between
     400 and 800 meters. No change to the EM is authorized.

Engagement Modifier–Table V

     5-29. The EM is the time in seconds required to achieve 100 points against the listed
     threat. To defeat the threat successfully, the crew must achieve the kill standard against
     the target and have an overall engagement time of no more than the EM plus 20 seconds.
     See TC 3-20.31 for the evaluation, timing, and scoring procedures.

Engagements–Table V

     5-30. Units are not allowed to deviate from any target type, target posture, range, and
     weapon system/ammunition type for Table V, Practice. (See figure 5-6 on page 180 for
     the breakdown of what target type, target posture, range and weapon system/ammunition
     type are used for each task number.)

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  179

     5-31. The throughput expectation of this table is 14 crews per day. Depending on
     maintenance or other unforeseen issues, recommend incorporating an additional half
     training day to the schedule. (See figure 5-7 for the expected throughput schedule based
     on the number of vehicles for firing and the unit’s day/night engagement breakdown for
     the engagements.)

Note. Time requirements overall are based on a single firing lane and include
4 hours of range maintenance down time (two blocks of 2 hours each). This
is standard at most facilities. These calculations are for planning purposes
only. Units that are more proficient in tower operations may have an
increased throughput.

TRAINING AIDS, DEVICES, SIMULATORS, AND SIMULATIONS–TABLE V

     5-33. This course requires the use of the CMU (some installations may refer to the CMU
     as the IPU) for all crews. The CMU is available on the digital range facility scheduled
     (if equipped). Coordination with the scheduled range facility manager will determine
     unit responsibilities for installation, troubleshooting, maintenance, and use of the
     devices ahead of the training day(s). If the range does not have a CMU available, unit
     may draw the CMUR from the TADSS warehouse. The CMUR differs from the CMU
     in that it stores all run data on a solid-state drive on the vehicle, for use with the provided
     AAR suite in the CMUR kit.

     5-34. The brigade commander must approve authorization for deviation from this
     requirement if TADSS are unavailable in writing. Units should focus on training and
     reinforcing the skills in the direct fire kill chain on this table specifically with their
     thermal optics, day and night to best prepare them for the follow-on training events.
     (See figure 5-9 for the TADSS required or recommended for Table V.)

Figure 5-9. Table V training aids, devices, simulators, and simulations
                                        requirements

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  183
