Chapter 5

PREPARE FOR TABLE V

     5-35. Units must coordinate for the appropriate training facility, equipment, targetry,
     training munitions, TADSS (if desired), and pyrotechnics to support Table V. During
     the preparation phase, the unit should ensure the proofing team, VCE’s, and other
     personnel receive training to standard to support their role as evaluators and trainers.
     This includes range officers in charge, range safety officers, and ammunition handlers.

TRAIN THE TRAINERS–TABLE V

     5-36. Typically, units have certified each VC and most gunners as VCEs prior entering
     the live-fire density. Units must have certified evaluators to ensure the training
     objectives for the training table are set and to provide effective AARs to each crew.
     Table V also requires experienced VCs to serve as the proofing team for the zeroing test
     (if necessary).

VEHICLE CREW EVALUATORS–TABLE V

     5-37. A trained and certified VCE team must be available to support prior to live fire to
     evaluate a gunnery density correctly. After certification, the unit must ensure sufficient
     VCEs are available to support the live-fire event. Evaluations of crew gunnery always
     come from outside the firing platoon element, and for qualification purposes, VCEs
     external to the battalion are required.

     5-38. Depending on the size of the weapons training density (make-up gunnery, platoon,
     company, or battalion) and the type of range facility used, the number of evaluators may
     vary. Having the correct number and type of evaluators per firing range maximizes the
     unit’s throughput of firing vehicles on the range, reduce range down time, and provide
     for the highest quality evaluations.

     5-39. Units provide the following to prepare the trainers:
          • Commander training objectives.
          • Conduct of the range briefing for the training event.
          • Provision of scenario and lane concept with crew tasks for training.
          • Risk assessment.
          • Identification of supporting doctrinal publications for use as primary
                references.
          • TADSS, pyrotechnics, blanks, and BESs used during the training event
                (see appendix A for the training munitions requirements for the complete
                training strategy).

184  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                                   Table V–Practice

RECONNAISSANCE OF THE SITE–TABLE V

     5-40. Commanders use an authorized training facility for Table V, Practice.
     Commanders follow the installation’s policies and procedures for formally scheduling
     the training location as far in advance as practical. See TC 3-20.31 for more in-depth
     information concerning range operations at the unit level.

     5-41. Once scheduled, the commander, Master Gunner, platoon leaders and platoon
     sergeants conduct a reconnaissance of the training areas to—

          • Verify sufficient space is available to support the entire unit through multiple
                training lanes.

          • Ensure multiple iterations or rotations through the scenario are achievable with
                appropriate retraining opportunities.

          • Ensure sufficient administrative areas are available to support group
                discussions, briefings, and AARs.

          • Provide sand table or terrain board.
          • Identify the ASP.
          • Determine the conduct of the range or the flow of training to maximize the live-

                fire range time.
          • Identify the range operating system and verify that the training scenarios have

                been properly loaded.
          • Schedule proofing of the range, based on the higher headquarters OPORD.
          • Ensure requested TADSS are available if provided by the range facility.
          • Issue the order.

     5-42. Commanders can issue an OPORD, WARNORD, or use the training schedule as
     necessary. The WARNORD should include the appropriate tasks, required leaders, and
     equipment needed, as well as the unit’s training timeline including walk-through,
     rehearsals, and AARs. WARNORDs for external organizations must include sufficient
     reaction time for the appropriate personnel and equipment needed.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  185


---

Chapter 5

REHEARSE–TABLE V

     5-43. The unit uses all training resources, materials, and equipment available to conduct
     rehearsals of training events. During the rehearsal, commanders ensure their unit has
     sufficient time to train to the specified standard including AARs and retraining time.
     Commanders ensure their unit verifies optional scenarios that increase complexity when
     warranted.

     5-44. Units should rehearse the training event from start to finish, and confirm the
     following:

          • The VCEs and proofing team understand the training scenarios and tasks.
          • Sufficient space is available to provide appropriate distances for engagements

                ranges and target dispersion.
          • Analog and digital systems are operational within the organization and to the

                higher headquarters regardless of their location.
          • Additional crew training packages (DA Form 8265, DA Form 8265-1, and

                appropriate zero documentation) are complete and provide for multiple
                iterations.
          • Sufficient administrative areas are available.
          • A review of AAR capabilities while encouraging a use of thru-sight video
                systems to increase the fidelity and quality of the AARs.
          • Location of retraining for crews as necessary.
          • Accessibility to crew simulator systems, plumb and synchronization ramps,
                tracking and manipulation panels, and additional boresighting locations are
                available.

     5-45. Once the unit completes the walk-through and training rehearsal, the commander
     makes any necessary adjustments to the plan and issues FRAGORDs as necessary.

186  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                                   Table V–Practice

EXECUTE TABLE V

     5-46. Once the unit arrives at the training location, the unit should—
          • Conduct a safety briefing for the training event.
          • Provide a conduct of the range briefing to establish the sequence of events for
                the training day.
          • Conduct sensitive item checks prior to executing training.
          • Assign VCE support as appropriate.
          • Issue the OPORD for the training mission.
          • Execute the training scenario.

     5-47. The training unit conducts informal AARs of each crew completing a phase of
     training within the training mission. During training, the assigned VCE’s, executive
     officer, 1SG, and Master Gunner review the subordinate unit’s performance. They
     follow the unit SOP, prescribed TTP, and the appropriate T&EOs, and review completed
     crew score sheets and roll-up documentation for clarity and completeness.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  187

     requires the crew to tactically move from the defilade to the enfilade while managing
     their switchology, ammunition type, and conduct of fire. This is one of the most complex
     engagements for the crew to master.

Chapter 5

ASSESS TABLE V

     5-58. The intent of the practice course of fire is to train the crews within the organization
     on the appropriate conduct of fire skills in both offensive and defensive postures against
     common threat arrays using the primary armament, coaxial, and commander’s machine
     gun. Proper evaluation and assessment of crew performance is key to improving
     individual and crew skills prior to moving to the qualification course of fire.

     5-59. Each crew should receive a formal AAR following the guidelines found in
     TC 3-20.31. Each evaluator should use DA Form 8265 and DA Form 8265-1 as the basis
     for their evaluation, identifying the firing occasional information from the scoresheet
     timelines. The evaluator and VC each review and sign the DA Form 8265-1.

             Note. VCEs must be certified or recertified as described in TC 3-20.31.
             Performing the duties, as a VCE during the conduct of Table V, Practice, is
             a significant part of building experience and knowledge of those gunners and
             VCs without firing.

     5-60. Units should track the various firing information from the score sheets to  identify
     where in the conduct of fire crews and the overall unit should apply additional   training
     effort. Some of those indicators are—

          • Target acquisition, detection, and identification.
          • Time to fire.
          • Time to kill.
          • Opening and closing times for machine gun engagements.
          • Gunner lay errors.
          • Scanning techniques.
          • Engagement techniques.
          • Conduct of fire issues.
          • Penalties by type.

208        TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025

RETRAIN–TABLE V

     5-61. The unit Master Gunner maintains the unit roll-up by engagement. A
     subcategorized roll-up by platoon provides the commander a snapshot of training trends,
     strengths, and weaknesses. The Master Gunner should not only identify crews that did
     not achieve a passing standard, but also crews that struggled on specific engagement
     tasks. Once done, the unit can establish and conduct the appropriate focused retraining
     followed by a subsequent evaluation.

     5-62. All crews successfully complete the course of fire based on the standards for the
     engagements listed earlier in this chapter.

             Note. All crews should successfully complete the course of fire based on the
             standards for the engagements listed earlier in this chapter. No crew should
             proceed to Table VI or any other live-fire event until they have achieved the
             minimum standard on Table V. The commander and Master Gunner may
             assess the crews’ capability to proceed without qualifying the table based on
             target acquisition, target kills, fire commands, and engagement techniques.

     5-63. Unit Master Gunners should include chair drills for the crews using the scenarios
     for each engagement for retraining purposes. Units should include future table’s
     engagements in the chair drills training to best prepare the crews for those events.
     Additional information on chair drills is in TC 3-20.31.

DRIVER’S TRAINING–TABLE V

     5-64. Leaders should determine the number of miles driven during the day and night
     phases for their assigned drivers. This information should be recorded, including the
     miles driven, miles driven with the enhanced viewer, and any tactical (off-road) miles
     driven.

CREW GUNNERY FOLDER RECORDS–TABLE V

     5-65. Once testing is complete, the Master Gunner records the results of all training
     completed in the crew gunnery folder. The records must be maintained for a minimum
     of 12 months.

                                      Chapter 6

                  Table VI–Qualification

    Crew Platform, Table VI, Qualification, is the record course of fire used to
    determine the live-fire proficiency of a crew platform. Table VI serves as
    the live-fire proficiency event for readiness reporting. It serves as the
    live-fire prerequisite for the crew to move to collective live-fire events.
    Table VI, Qualification, is a live environment-training event under live-fire
    conditions using full-caliber training ammunition, realistic targetry and
    engagement scenarios, at a combat tempo, conducted on an authorized
    live-fire facility. Regardless of crew platform type, this table reinforces the
    basic skills trained during previous events but increases the tempo to
    expected engagement sequence capabilities to build leader and element
    tactical and technical mastery.

OVERVIEW OF TABLE VI

     6-1. Table VI, Qualification, is from the crew’s vehicle and uses full-caliber training
     ammunition on an authorized live-fire facility or safety certified training area that
     assesses an element’s tactical employment proficiency and lethality. Table VI trains and
     evaluates the crew’s ability to engage stationary or moving targets placed in a tactical
     array, during day and limited visibility conditions, with an operational or simulated
     degraded fire control system, as applicable. Crews execute these tasks from the offense
     and defense using full-caliber ammunition.

     6-2. This table requires each crew to successfully complete the live-fire prerequisites,
     all zeroing procedures for their platform, and complete the defensive engagements on
     Table IV. This table is designed to train and evaluate complex engagement skills, day
     and night, while fighting from the defense and during offensive engagements. It is the
     qualification event for BFV crews.

                              Table VI, Qualification

          This event builds unit proficiency and is a requirement to build unit
          readiness.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  217


---

Chapter 6

     6-3. Figure 6-1 shows where Table VI, Qualification, resides within the BFV training
     strategy through crew qualification. Table VI is the qualification event where the crew
     is evaluated on their demonstrated proficiency executing critical live-fire main gun,
     machine gun, and complex change of weapons system engagements from the offense
     and defense, during the day and night phases. It requires the crew to demonstrate their
     ability to effectively employ the BFV’s weapons using fully mission capable fire control
     system components as well as redundant, degraded engagements.

     Figure 6-1. Table VI, Qualification

218  TC 3-20.31-25                        23 July 2025


---

                                                                                           Table VI–Qualification

TABLE VI PURPOSE

     6-4. Table VI is a skills assessment and risk mitigation tool for the commander and is
     the platform’s qualification for reporting purposes. It is also a proficiency gate the crew
     must successfully complete prior to entering any collective live-fire event. It assists the
     commander in determining the safe and effective use of the platform, its weapons,
     identification of threats and common ammunition by the crewmembers in the unit.
     Further, it identifies shortfalls and training gaps in those gunnery specific skills, which
     build crew proficiency and unit readiness.

TABLE VI METHOD

     6-5. Table VI is a performance-based evaluation of basic and advanced gunnery skills
     to the Army-standard of knowledge or skill in a live environment under live-fire
     conditions. Conduct of Table VI is on vehicles using full-caliber ammunition, and BESs.
     It utilizes experienced evaluators to educate, train, test, and retrain Soldiers to meet or
     exceed those standards.

TABLE VI END STATE

     6-6. The VC/gunner assigned as a crew have successfully demonstrated the skills and
     knowledge required to continue training on the platform through platform qualification.
     Commanders are confident in their Soldier’s abilities in the position assigned. The tasks
     evaluated in Table VI provide the commander a means to certify the crew’s basic and
     advanced weapons platform proficiency prior to collective live-fire events. The
     commander and Master Gunner use the results from the qualification course when
     structuring the unit’s crew training program and is a readiness reportable item.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  219


---

Chapter 6

TABLE VI TASK, CONDITIONS, AND STANDARD

     6-7. Units may not add to the live-fire requirements or tasks associated with Table VI.
     The division commander or equivalent maintains the qualification course of fire
     standards and approved scenarios. (See figure 6-2 for the task, conditions, and standard
     for Table VI, Qualification.)

                               Figure 6-2. Table VI, Qualification
             Note. If any individual target score on an engagement is less than 70 points,
             the engagement is unqualified, regardless of overall engagement score.

220  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025

                                                                                           Table VI–Qualification

PLAN FOR TABLE VI

     6-8. Table VI, Qualification, is a live-fire training environment event using training aids,
     devices, and simulators, and includes the use of full-caliber and small arms training
     ammunition and BESs. The qualification course of fire requires external VCEs and
     coordination with the higher headquarters for support.

     6-9. This section details the resources needed for planning purposes. It includes general
     information on the following topics for consideration:

          • Uniform—the minimum requirements for the combat vehicle crewmember’s
                fire-retardant clothing and protective equipment.

          • Facilities—available facilities that support the training event.
          • Training plan—the minimum tasks, general guidelines, and special instructions

                for the training event.
          • Time and throughput—the estimated time required to complete the training

                event including throughput considerations for planning.
          • Ammunition—any training munitions required to complete the training event.
          • TADSS—any available training aids that directly support or augment the

                training event.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  221

Chapter 6

TABLE VI UNIFORM

     6-10. Crews must be in the appropriate vehicle crewmember uniform or prescribed unit
     uniform and protective mask. Crews are not required to have any additional body armor,
     ballistic plates, or gear during the training event unless directed by the commander.
     (See figure 6-3 for the minimum uniform training requirements for this event.)

     Figure 6-3. Table VI uniform requirements

TABLE VI FACILITIES

     6-11. Table VI requires the use of a maneuver gunnery range that allows live fire for
     small and large caliber ammunition. Master Gunners must be familiar with the family
     of maneuver ranges, the range operations control area, and the specific instrumentation
     and target sets available on the range facility selected for Table VI. (The maneuver range
     facilities recommended as primary and alternate facilities are shown in figure 6-4.)

                           Thru-Sight Video Required

          Units are required to utilize the thru-sight video system available for
          their platform on the qualification event. This is to ensure the highest
          fidelity AAR, and take-home packages are created for the crews
          and commanders to build future unit training plans.

TABLE VI RANGE SUPPORT

     6-12. The range support group provides VCE teams to evaluate the course of fire. This
     requires all gunners and above to certify or recertify as VCEs between T-6 and
     T-execution of the training event.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  223

Chapter 6

     6-13. This includes the most common support required for crew platform live-fire
     events. In the event the unit utilizes multiple facilities to complete the zero and defensive
     engagements, appropriate adjustments should be made to this general structure.
     (See figure 6-5 for the range support personnel general requirements.)

            Figure 6-5. Range Support personnel requirements, example

TABLE VI TRAINING PLAN

     6-14. Table VI, Qualification, provides a challenging scenario to the crews to prepare
     them for collective live-fire events and deployment. The qualification course exercises
     the crew’s conduct of fire and engagement skills in a demanding manner that
     demonstrates the minimum proficiency of the crew.

     6-15. Units are required to use full-scale targets at full range for the engagement
     scenarios unless specifically directed. To maintain training standards across the Army,
     there is no authorization for deviation from the engagements on Table VI beyond the
     guidelines listed in TC 3-20.31 for alternate targetry.

224  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                           Table VI–Qualification

     6-16. The design of Table VI is purposefully challenging to the crews while providing
     realistic engagements that exercise the crew’s ability to utilize the key capabilities of the
     vehicle’s fire control system. Units retrain as necessary with full-caliber ammunition
     using first-round hit savings (harvested ammunition) from the previous tables in the
     training strategy. Units must manage the available resources to ensure all crews
     successfully complete Table VI before proceeding to any collective live-fire event or
     deployment.

     6-17. All crew platform tables are multiple qualification criteria tables. Crews must
     score at least 70 points per target according to the target kill standards in TC 3-20.31 on
     single and multiple target engagements to qualify the engagement. Crews must also
     score more than 700 points overall with a minimum of 70 points per target during 7 of 10
     engagements on the qualification table to be used. Crews must qualify at least one
     engagement during day and one engagement during limited visibility to be qualified.

     6-18. Table VI uses full-caliber training ammunition on an authorized live-fire facility
     or safety certified training area that assesses an element’s tactical employment
     proficiency and lethality.

     6-19. Crews must successfully complete the qualification course prior to executing any
     collective live-fire training. Units must follow the training guidelines for those gates in
     the appropriate training circular. Units may conduct make-up qualifications prior to live
     collective training when required but units must accomplish live-fire prerequisites as
     directed.

Table VI Scenario Requirements

     6-20. This table is designed to train and evaluate the crew’s ability to engage stationary
     or moving targets placed in a tactical array, from the offense and defense, during day
     and limited visibility conditions.

     6-21. All engagements are evaluated using certified VCE’s using DA Form 8265 and
     DA Form 8265-1 as the official record of training. Master Gunners must ensure the
     training records are included in each crew’s gunnery packet (see TC 3-20.31).

     6-22. The engagements listings are by capability type using the standard engagement
     naming structure listed in TC 3-20.31. It provides the requirements for each engagement,
     which are listed in paragraphs 6-23 through 6-30.

Task Description–Table VI

     6-23. The task description includes both the engagement number (the table number plus
     engagement task) followed by the system capability trained.

Firing Vehicle Posture–Table VI

     6-24. The firing vehicle posture establishes the offense or defense condition for the
     engagement.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  225


---

Chapter 6

Firer–Table VI
     6-25. The firer column identifies which crewmember is to engage the target listed.
     Targets defeated by a different crewmember listed as the firer result in an auto-zero
     penalty, failing to adhere to course conditions.

Weapon (or Ammunition)–Table VI
     6-26. Identifies the weapon (coaxial or primary armament ammunition type) that is used
     to defeat the threat. Design of each engagement is for a specific weapon or ammunition
     type (primary armament) for use, which relates to the crew conduct of fire, optic
     selection, engagement techniques, or other unique implied tasks. Crews that fail to
     announce the appropriate weapon or ammunition, or appropriate selection for
     subsequent or supplemental engagements may be penalized IAW TC 3-20.31.

Target Type–Table VI
     6-27. The target type is the target required for the presentation, IAW TC 25-8.

Target Posture–Table VI
     6-28. The target posture establishes if the target is stationary, moving, or evasive for the
     engagement. See TC 3-20.31 for more information on target postures.

Range (to Target)–Table VI
     6-29. The range column is the required range (full scale or equivalent scaled range) from
     the firing vehicle to the target. For offensive engagements, this distance calculation is
     from the center of the movement box to the target. The range to target distance is
     provided in meters, (for example, 600). The target may be placed at the distance
     indicated, plus or minus 200 meters. For example, the target may be placed between
     400 and 800 meters. No change to the EM is authorized.

Engagement Modifier–Table VI
     6-30. The EM is the time in seconds required to achieve 100 points against the listed
     threat. To defeat the threat successfully, the crew must achieve the kill standard against
     the target and have an overall engagement time of no more than the EM plus 20 seconds.
     See TC 3-20.31 for the evaluation, timing, and scoring procedures.

Engagements

     6-31. Units are not allowed to deviate from any target type, target posture, range, and
     weapon system/ammunition. (See figure 6-6 for the breakdown of what target type,
     target posture, range, and weapon system/ammunition type used for each task number.)

Chapter 6

TRAINING AIDS, DEVICES, SIMULATORS, AND SIMULATIONS–TABLE VI

     6-34. This course requires the use of the CMU (some installations may refer to the CMU
     as an IPU) for all crews. The CMU is available on the digital range facility scheduled
     (if equipped). Coordination with the scheduled range facility manager will determine
     unit responsibilities for installation, troubleshooting, maintenance, and use of the
     devices ahead of the training day(s). If the range does not have a CMU available, unit
     may draw the CMUR from the TADSS warehouse. The CMUR differs from the CMU
     in that it stores all run data on a solid-state drive on the vehicle, for use with the provided
     AAR suite in the CMUR kit.

     6-35. The brigade commander must approve authorization for deviation from this
     requirement if TADSS are unavailable. Units should focus on training and reinforcing
     the skills in the direct fire kill chain on this table specifically with their thermal optics,
     day and night to best prepare them for the follow-on training events. (Figure 6-9 shows
     the TADSS required or recommended for Table VI.)

     Figure 6-9. Table VI training aids, devices, simulators, and simulations
                                              requirements

230  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                           Table VI–Qualification

PREPARE TABLE VI

     6-36. Units must coordinate for the appropriate training facility, equipment, targetry,
     training munitions, TADSS (if desired), and pyrotechnics to support Table VI. During
     the preparation phase, the unit should ensure the proofing team, VCE’s, and other
     personnel receive training to standard to support their role as evaluators and trainers.
     This includes range officers in charge, range safety officers, and ammunition handlers.

TRAIN THE TRAINERS–TABLE VI

     6-37. Typically, units have certified each VC/gunner as VCEs prior entering the live fire
     density. Units must have certified evaluators to ensure the training objectives for the
     training table are set and to provide effective AARs to each crew. Table VI also requires
     experienced VC/gunners to serve as the proofing team for the zero test.

VEHICLE CREW EVALUATORS–TABLE VI

     6-38. A trained and certified VCE team must be available to support prior to live fire to
     evaluate a gunnery density correctly. After certification, the unit must ensure sufficient
     VCEs are available to support the live-fire event. Evaluations of crew gunnery always
     come from outside the firing platoon element, and for qualification purposes, VCEs
     external to the battalion are required.

     6-39. Depending on the size of the weapons training density (make-up gunnery, platoon,
     company, or battalion) and the type of range facility used, the number of evaluators may
     vary. Having the correct number and type of evaluators per firing range maximizes the
     unit’s throughput of firing vehicles on the range, reduce range down time, and provide
     for the highest quality evaluations. See TC 3-20.31 for more in-depth information on
     VCEs, the evaluation team, and the evaluation process.

     6-40. Units provide the following to prepare the trainers:
          • Commander training objectives.
          • Conduct of the range briefing for the training event.
          • Scenario and lane concept with crew tasks for training.
          • Risk assessment.
          • Identification of supporting doctrinal publications for use as primary
                references.
          • TADSS, pyrotechnics, and BESs used during the training event
                (see appendix A for the training munitions requirements for the complete
                training strategy).

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  231


---

Chapter 6

RECONNAISSANCE OF THE SITE–TABLE VI

     6-41. Commanders use an authorized training facility for Table VI, Qualification.
     Commanders follow the installation’s policies and procedures for formally scheduling
     the training location as far in advance as practical. See TC 3-20.31 for more in-depth
     information concerning range operations at the unit level.

     6-42. Once scheduled, the commander, Master Gunner, platoon leaders and platoon
     sergeants conduct a reconnaissance of the training areas to—

          • Verify sufficient space is available to support the entire unit through multiple
                training lanes.

          • Ensure multiple iterations or rotations through the scenario are achievable with
                appropriate retraining opportunities.

          • Ensure sufficient administrative areas are available to support group
                discussions, briefings, and AARs.

          • Provide a sand table or terrain board.
          • Identify the ASP.
          • Determine the conduct of the range or the flow of training to maximize the

                live-fire range time.
          • Identify the range operating system and verify that the training scenarios have

                been properly loaded.
          • Schedule proofing of the range, based on the higher headquarters OPORD.
          • Ensure requested TADSS are available if provided by the range facility.

ISSUE THE ORDER–TABLE VI

     6-43. Commanders can issue an OPORD, WARNORD, or use the training schedule as
     necessary. The WARNORD should include the appropriate tasks, required leaders, and
     equipment needed, as well as the unit’s training timeline including walk-through,
     rehearsals, and AARs. WARNORDs for external organizations must include sufficient
     reaction time for the appropriate personnel and equipment needed.

REHEARSE–TABLE VI

     6-44. The unit uses all training resources, materials, and equipment available to conduct
     rehearsals of training events. During the rehearsal, commanders ensure their unit has
     sufficient time to train to the specified standard including AARs and retraining time.
     Commanders ensure their unit verifies optional scenarios that increase complexity when
     warranted.

     6-45. Units should rehearse the training event from start to finish, and confirm the
     following:

          • The VCEs and proofing team understand the training scenarios and tasks.
          • Sufficient space is available to provide appropriate distances for engagements

                ranges and target dispersion.
          • Analog and digital systems are operational within the organization and to the

                higher headquarters regardless of their location.
          • Additional crew training packages (DA Form 8265 and appropriate computer

                correction factors [zero] documentation) are complete and provide for multiple
                iterations.

232        TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                           Table VI–Qualification

          • Sufficient administrative areas are available.
          • AAR capabilities are reviewed, while encouraging use of through the sight

                video systems to increase the fidelity and quality of the AARs.
          • Location of retraining for crews as necessary.
          • Accessibility to crew simulator systems, plumb and synchronization ramps,

                tracking and manipulation panels, and additional boresighting locations are
                available.

     6-46. Once the unit completes the walk-through and training rehearsal, the commander
     makes any necessary adjustments to the plan and issues FRAGORDs as necessary.

EXECUTE TABLE VI

     6-47. Once the unit arrives at the training location, they should—
          • Conduct a safety briefing for the training event.
          • Provide a conduct of the range briefing to establish the sequence of events for
                the training day.
          • Conduct sensitive item checks prior to executing training.
          • Receive briefing from the external VCE team.
          • Establish the firing order.
          • Identify the maintenance support area for any fire control or weapons issues on
                the range.
          • Install TADSS as required.

     6-48. The training unit conducts informal AARs of each crew completing a phase of
     training within the training mission. During training, the commander, executive officer,
     1SG, and Master Gunner review the crew’s performance. They follow the unit SOP,
     prescribed TTP, and the appropriate T&EOs, and review completed crew score sheets
     and roll-up documentation for clarity and completeness.

             Note. The sequence of the engagements as well as which engagements are
             fired in a chemical environment are determined as unit policy directs.

Chapter 6

ASSESS TABLE VI

     6-59. The intent of the qualification course of fire is to train the crews within the
     organization on the appropriate conduct of fire skills during offensive and defensive
     postures against common threat arrays using the primary armament, coaxial, and
     commander’s machine gun. Proper evaluation and assessment of crew performance is
     key to improving individual and crew skills prior to moving to collective live-fire events.

     6-60. Each crew should receive a formal AAR following the guidelines found in
     TC 3-20.31. Each evaluator should use DA Form 8265 and DA Form 8265-1 as the basis
     for their evaluation, identifying the firing occasional information from the scoresheet
     timelines. The evaluator and VC each review and sign the DA Form 8265-1.

             Note. Certified VCEs, externally evaluate Table VI, Qualification. Units
             coordinate with their higher headquarters for those personnel. Typically, the
             brigade combat team will train and certify those evaluators. If there is no
             external unit that can meet the requirements for the primary evaluator
             (see TC 3-20.31). Brigade coordination outside the division may be required
             to provide the appropriate personnel to evaluate the crews. When that level
             of support is not feasible, the division commander (or equivalent) must
             authorize self-evaluation using senior subject matter experts.

     6-61. Units should track the various firing information from the score sheets to  identify
     where in the conduct of fire crews and the overall unit should apply additional   training
     effort. Some of those indicators are—

          • Target acquisition, detection, and identification.
          • Time to fire.
          • Time to kill.
          • Opening and closing times for machine gun engagements.
          • Gunner lay errors.
          • Scanning techniques.
          • Engagement techniques.
          • Conduct of fire issues.
          • Penalties by type.

254        TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025

RETRAIN–TABLE VI

     6-62. The unit Master Gunner maintains the unit roll-up by engagement. A
     subcategorized roll-up by platoons provides the commander a snapshot of training
     trends, strengths, and weaknesses. The Master Gunner should not only identify crews
     that did not achieve a passing standard, but also crews that struggled on specific
     engagement tasks. Once done, the unit can establish and conduct the appropriate focused
     retraining followed by a subsequent evaluation.

     6-63. All crews must successfully complete the course of fire based on the standards for
     the engagements listed earlier in this chapter. No crew should proceed to a collective
     live-fire event until they have achieved the minimum standard on Table VI,
     Qualification.

     6-64. Unit Master Gunners should include chair drills for the crews using the scenarios
     for each engagement for retraining purposes. Additional information on chair drills is in
     TC 3-20.31.

     6-65. Any crew that disqualified for any reason may not refire without brigade
     commander approval after corrective training is complete. See chapter 7 for additional
     information on disqualified crews.

DRIVER’S TRAINING–TABLE VI

     6-66. Leaders should determine the number of miles driven during the day and night

     driven.

CREW GUNNERY FOLDER RECORDS–TABLE VI

     6-67. Once testing is complete, the Master Gunner records the results of all training

                                      Chapter 7

                 Qualification Standards

    The crew tables culminate in the evaluation of the crew’s ability to destroy
    threat targets using full-caliber ammunition both day and night. Successful
    completion of Table VI, Qualification is required for crews to advance to
    collective live-fire training.
    Crew Table VI is the record course of fire used to determine the live-fire
    proficiency of a crew platform. Table VI serves as the commander’s
    assessment of lethality and proficiency of the crew platform. This
    qualification is required by AR 350-1 and described as a metric of unit
    readiness within AR 220-1. Crew platform qualification is the cornerstone
    of company, battalion/squadron, and brigade combat team readiness.

OVERVIEW OF QUALIFICATION STANDARDS

     7-1. Crew Table VI, Qualification is the Army’s standard demonstrations of a BFV
     crew’s employment proficiency of their platform during a live-fire event. Crews must
     successfully complete Table VI, Qualification to progress to higher echelon live-fire
     collective events. Crew qualification is also used as prerequisites for additional training,
     certifications, and functional courses.

     7-2. All crew platform tables include multiple qualification criteria to determine when a
     crew is qualified. The multiple qualification criteria are not waiverable on the
     qualification table.

                                   Qualified Crew

          A qualified crew is a VC and gunner combination that successfully
          completes Crew Platform, Table VI, Qualification and have not met
          any of the condemnation criteria of the qualification period.

TERMS OF REFERENCE

     7-3. Units must be familiar with the training terminology used to describe the various
     components of proficiency, qualification, and readiness. The terms found in paragraphs
     7-4 through 7-10 are used within this chapter when discussing qualification.

QUALIFICATION

     7-4. A qualified crew is a VC and gunner combination that successfully completes crew
     platform, Table VI, Qualification and have not met any of the condemnation criteria of
     the qualification period.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  263


---

Chapter 7

QUALIFICATION DURATION

     7-5. The length of time a qualification is valid provided all other qualification conditions
     exist. For crew platforms, the qualification duration is 12 months.

PREREQUISITES

     7-6. Training events prior to live-fire that must be successfully completed by the Soldier
     or crew. Official records of the training must be maintained for a minimum of 12 months.

EXTERNAL EVALUATION

     7-7. Qualification events require external evaluations by certified VCEs. Evaluators are
     typically external to the battalion/squadron (see figure 7-1). For some units in remote
     locations, the unit must conduct the evaluations with trained leaders within the
     organization. In those circumstances, the evaluator for any crew should not have
     influence over the promotion potential or be in any professional competition with the
     VC or gunner.

                       Figure 7-1. External evaluation requirements

QUALIFICATION PERIOD

     7-8. The period that begins the day a qualification is awarded and continues for
     365 days. This term is used to describe the time since qualification occurred.

CONDEMNATION CRITERIA

     7-9. The rules that determine if any given qualification is still valid. For crew platforms,
     these rules are time, key leader turn over, and commander assessment.

MULTIPLE QUALIFICATION CRITERIA

     7-10. The rules used to crew platform live-fire to determine their qualification status or
     rating based on their performance during Table VI engagements.

264  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                         Qualification Standards

QUALIFICATION STANDARDS

     7-11. Crew Table VI, Qualification is the record courses of fire used to determine the
     live-fire proficiency of a BFV crew. Table VI serves as the commander’s assessment of
     lethality and proficiency of the crew on their platform. This qualification is required by
     AR 350-1 and described as a metric of unit readiness within AR 220-1. Crew platform
     qualification is the cornerstone of company, battalion/squadron, and brigade combat
     team readiness.

     7-12. Crew Table VI, Qualification is the Army’s standard demonstration of a crew’s
     employment proficiency the crew platform during a live-fire event. Crews must
     successfully complete Table VI, Qualification to progress to higher echelon live-fire
     collective events. Crew qualification is also used as prerequisites for additional training,
     certifications, and functional courses. Certified, experienced leaders external to the unit
     evaluate crew platforms on the qualification course of fire.

     7-13. Crew qualification uses multiple-qualification criteria to determine the live-fire
     proficiency. To qualify on Table VI, the qualification course of fire, the crew must at a
     minimum successfully—

          • Qualify 7 of 10 engagements by achieving a minimum of 70 points on all
                targets presented within the engagement overall.

          • Score a minimum of 700 points out of a possible 1,000 points, overall.
          • Qualify a minimum of one day engagement and one night engagement.

     7-14. Table M, Missile Qualification uses multiple-qualification criteria to determine
     the missile proficiency. If units elect to conduct Table M, the missile qualification course
     of fire, the crew must at a minimum successfully—

          • Qualify 7 of 10 engagements by achieving a minimum of 70 points on all
                targets presented within the engagement overall.

          • Score a minimum of 700 points out of a possible 1,000 points, overall.
          • Qualify a minimum of one day engagement and one night engagement.
          • If TOW-on-the-move equipped, conduct one offensive TOW engagement, day

                and night.

     7-15. With proper training and adherence to the training strategy, units should achieve
     qualification on the first run attempt (termed first run) on a minimum of 90 percent of
     their crews. Units that fail to achieve 90 percent qualified crews on their first run,
     commonly known as Q1, indicate a failure to train the necessary skills at the
     company/troop level.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  265


---

Chapter 7

     7-16. Qualification standards for BFV crews establish the reporting requirements for
     crew platforms for readiness reporting (see figure 7-2).

     Figure 7-2. Crew platform qualification standards

266  TC 3-20.31-25                                      23 July 2025


---

                                                                                         Qualification Standards

CREW PLATFORM RATINGS

     7-17. Crew evaluation basis is on their level of competence on their platform using the
     standard evaluation procedures as described in the platform’s training publication. The
     end state for each crew after completing Table VI and Table M, is a crew proficient on
     all systems within their platform, that is confident and competent to a standard that best
     prepares them for combat.

     7-18. Crews will receive an overall qualification rating for Table VI, Crew Qualification
     and Table M, Missile Qualification, based on the multiple-qualification criteria
     (see figure 7-3). Commanders are authorized to award crewmembers the appropriate
     weapons qualification badge based on the live-fire proficiency event rating including
     the corresponding qualification clasp.

Figure 7-3. Crew platform qualification badges authorized

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25                                267


---

Chapter 7

     7-19. When units require multiple attempts to qualify, units traditionally classify them
     by the maximum number of iterations of any engagement on the course of fire
     (see figure 7-4).

                       Figure 7-4. Qualifying with multiple attempts
     7-20. The Q number ratings are used to identify weak performing crews that require
     additional training to achieve the appropriate readiness level expected of their MOS,
     assigned position, and unit readiness. They provide a rapid indication to the commander
     beyond the crew’s score where training deficiencies or gaps are present.

             Note. For example, a crew refires the 62 engagement 3 times on Table VI to
             achieve the required 700 minimum points with 7 qualified engagements. As
             the 62 engagement was fired multiple times (three in this case), the crew is
             rated qualified as Q3.

268  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                   Qualification Standards

7-21. Units that train according to the training strategy throughout the training period
with proactive leadership involvement will achieve the desired training outcomes. To
do so, these units typically achieve the benchmarks (see figure 7-5).

 Figure 7-5. Training benchmarks achieved by following the training strategy

QUALIFICATION REFIRES

     7-22. If a crew fails to qualify, they must refire select engagements for qualification.
     The highest rating that a crew can earn when refiring is qualified.

     7-23. A crew refires only the engagements that they failed to achieve the minimum
     standard. Units should initially focus on those nonqualified engagements that provide
     the most points to gain. A crew refires only the number of engagements required to
     obtain a qualified rating.

     7-24. AR 220-1 and AR 350-1 delineate the qualification standards reporting
     requirements. They relate directly to the individual and collective training proficiency
     goals of the readiness model found in AR 525-29.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  269


---

Chapter 7

DISQUALIFICATION

     7-25. A disqualification is the immediate removal from live training of any unsafe crew
     until the crew satisfactorily completes a recertification program. Disqualification is a
     critical failure in the safe conduct of the range by leaders using weapons or weapon
     systems in such a manner that has the potential to injure or kill Soldiers or civilians, or
     excessive damage to government property.

     7-26. The following is a list of disqualification criteria:
          • A crew that performs any actions listed in the immediate disqualification
                section of DA Form 8265-1.
          • A crew that performs three each 30-point penalties as listed on
                DA Form 8265-1 during the same table.
          • A crewmember or entire crew can be disqualified and require recertification
                for actions not associated with firing.
                 The unit commander may disqualify individuals or crews that consistently
                     perform unsafe acts during non-firing or preparation tasks.
                 This type of disqualification will be at the discretion of the unit
                     commander.

     7-27. Any crew disqualified for any reason may not refire without brigade commander
     approval after corrective training is complete. Corrective training for disqualified crews
     includes, at a minimum, repeating and successful completion of the following:

          • Table I, GST.
          • Table II, STX-V GTLF on the authorized training system.
          • Table III, STX.

     7-28. Disqualified crews indicate a significant lack of awareness for safety on the
     vehicle. The requirement for retraining on Tables I, II, and III, at a minimum, may be
     waived by the first general officer in the chain of command and may not be delegated.

             Note. The battalion or squadron commander is the approving authority for all
             recertification. A commander may impose additional retraining requirements
             as desired to ensure the crew’s safety and effective performance during
             live-fire training.

270  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                         Qualification Standards

SUSTAINING CREW PROFICIENCY

     7-29. Once the element has successfully completed their qualification event, the unit
     must consider how the condemnation criteria impact their subordinate’s proficiency.
     The following paragraphs describes how crew platforms maintain their proficiency, how
     to identify when a proficiency rating has triggered one or more condemnation criteria,
     and any options the commander can consider lessening or mitigating those impacts.

CREW PLATFORM CONDEMNATION CRITERIA

     7-30. Qualifications remain valid until one of the condemnation criteria is met. The
     qualification condemnation criteria for crew platforms are found in paragraphs
     7-31 through 7-34.

Time

     7-31. The maximum qualification duration for any qualification, certification, or
     proficiency rating is 12 months since the previous qualification, certification, or
     proficiency rating.

Key Leaders

     7-32. The minimum identified key leaders on the crew platform (VC and gunner for the
     BFV) are no longer assigned to their qualified position.

Commander Assessment

     7-33. Any commander within the chain of command determines the crew no longer
     maintains crew platform proficiency for any reason. Any condemnation based on the
     commander’s assessment will instigate immediate retraining.

               No alterations to the condemnation criteria
                                      authorized

          Units may not alter, change, or modify the condemnation criteria.
          Evaluation commanders have the option, based on their
          assessment of the element, to condemn any proficiency rating they
          are authorized to assign.

     7-34. Once any crew loses their qualification status, that crew must be removed from
     the battle roster. Once removed from the battle roster, commanders and their advisors
     review the potential to maintain the remaining crewmembers on either the crew roster
     or cross-train roster based on each situation.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  271


---

Chapter 7

TALENT MANAGEMENT

     7-35. Commanders can mitigate the impacts to their unit’s proficiency in a variety of
     methods. Talent management skills before, during, and after conducting the crew
     platform training strategy is required by leaders to ensure the maximum proficiency is
     maintained and sustained within the larger organization. Crew platform qualification has
     a direct impact on the unit’s overall readiness and contribute to the unit’s level of
     proficiency.

     7-36. To best manage crew turbulence when establishing crew rosters, the commander
     and staff consider the process described in paragraphs 7-37 through 7-45.

Identify

     7-37. Identify high-performing Soldiers that demonstrate technical and tactical
     understanding and who are prepared for increased responsibility. Consider their
     previous assignments and experience for potential assignment as a gunner or VC.
     Identify the Soldier’s year and month available or date of expected return from overseas
     assignment for permanent change of station. Soldiers are determined for eligibility for
     stabilization following established Army personnel policies.

Assess

     7-38. Units must assess new Soldier/crewmember proficiency on their platform.
     Commanders that receive new leaders who achieved proficiency within the last
     qualification period have assessment options to maintain element qualifications.
     Commanders can assess two previously qualified key leaders from different elements to
     determine their proficiency status if they are assigned together in an element. This is the
     least preferred method of talent management and requires the commander to make
     assessments using previous crew records, sustainment training in simulations, and other
     assessments to determine their proficiency status.

Assign

     7-39. The VC/gunner combinations are selected together. This maximizes the key
     leader’s longevity and future proficiency ratings in a more stable manner. Each
     crewmember is assigned to their position on their potential and longevity. Those leaders
     are placed in the next higher position (for example, gunner to VC, or driver to gunner).

Transfer

     7-40. When promotions occur, commanders should make every effort to keep the trained
     key leaders together, including inter-battalion transfers. Commanders should consider
     moving the newly promoted key leader and key leader combination together where the
     gaining unit within the battalion will benefit from maintaining proficient key leaders.

272  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                         Qualification Standards

Cross-train

     7-41. Experienced Soldiers are easier to train than new Soldiers. Units should utilize
     harvested training ammunition to train and qualify alternate firers to build experience.
     Units should invest first-round-hit ammunition savings, commonly referred to as
     ammunition harvesting, to cross training young leaders in key leader positions. In doing
     so, commanders can mitigate key leader turnover with trained and proficient leaders in
     stride with key leader turbulence. Ammunition available through first round hit savings
     vary from training event to training event. Units must be prepared to execute training
     based on the varying training munitions availability and plan for additional time on
     range facilities to build depth in their trained and qualified cross-trained crews.

Stabilize

     7-42. Commanders must ensure personnel administration is involved in crew
     management through requesting stabilization for Soldiers or organizations in unique
     situations. Some of these situations are noted in paragraphs 7-43 through 7-45.

Functional Course Graduation

     7-43. Soldier attendance to the various platform mastery courses or instructor courses
     provide units with a graduate stabilization capability. These types of requests maximize
     the unit’s investment in the Soldier’s by effectively stabilizing the Soldier with the unit
     for up to 24 months.

Stop Move

     7-44. Units request to stabilize specific Soldiers, small unit, or an entire organization six
     months prior to latest arrival date. This does not apply to Soldiers already on assignment
     instructions. The stop move is effective 180 days prior to the unit’s latest arrival date
     and continues through redeployment to at least 90 days.

Oversight

     7-45. The special actions branch at Army Human Resources Command provides
     oversight and action against all stabilization, stop movement, and crew stabilization
     requests. For units that have an assigned latest arrival date the brigade commander
     requests stabilization for those VC and gunner combinations they foresee becoming
     turbulent in the next four quarters. For additional information, see the Army Human
     Resources Command website.

Plan for Turbulence

     7-46. Commanders, with the assistance of their Master Gunner must anticipate future
     turbulence before it happens. As the key leader turbulence periods occur (permanent
     change of station, date of expected return from overseas assignment, reenlistment
     options, promotion potential, and so forth), the commander must be able to visualize and
     identify the most common causes, their impacts on proficiency and readiness, and
     identify the most probable points of failure in sustaining readiness.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  273


---

Chapter 7

CREW PLATFORM TURBULENCE

     7-47. The commander may consider a turbulent crew as qualified (proficient) in extreme
     circumstances. Commanders accept the risk when assigning turbulent crewmembers
     together and carry that crew as qualified for reporting purposes. It is the least preferred
     method of crew management and is approved by the first general officer in their chain
     of command.

     7-48. The commander should review the cohesion and proficiency of critical skills on
     the platform used by the VC and gunner. Such cohesion and proficiency are
     demonstrated by the crews’—

          • Previous qualification in their assigned position on a different crew within the
                previous qualification period (12 months).

          • Successful completion of Table I, GST, for the unit within the previous
                qualification period or on a diagnostic exam.

          • Completion of the simulations training strategy from entry-level through the
                GTLF exercise (which typically takes between 8 to 12 hours in the simulator
                with a qualified instructor/operator and platoon sergeant oversight).

          • Successful completion of the crew platform, Table II, STX–V GTLF with a
                score of 850 or above.

     7-49. If commanders are not completely confident in the crew cohesion, capability,
     experience, and performance, they must rate them as unqualified the next training
     density.

274  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                         Qualification Standards

CREW TURBULENCE AND QUALIFICATION

     7-50. There are only two key leaders on the BFV. Units must develop talent
     management skills to maintain the crew’s qualification status and proficiency as long as
     possible. (See figure 7-6 for an example of a proficient Bradley crew.)

              Figure 7-6. Bradley proficient crew example

Chapter 7

     7-55. The commander must understand what they can, should, and should not do to
     mitigate crew turbulence. When assessing turbulent crews, the commander should make
     use of the authorized simulations systems, GSTs, the VCE course, and other training
     assets. See the turbulent crew section in this chapter for additional information.

              Assignments with Qualified Rating Approval
                                       Authority

          Commanders should not carry turbulent crews without previous live
          fire demonstrated proficiency in position. Providing as much trigger
          time on the system as possible, as often as possible, will create less
          difficult talent management decisions.

                                 Commander Risk

          Carrying a turbulent crew as qualified is a commander option. The
          commander accepts the risk to the crew and small unit when
          reporting readiness for a crew in this manner.
          Approval to carry the crew as qualified resides with the first general
          officer in the chain of command.

                                     Appendix A

               Ammunition Requirements

    The BFV ammunition requirements, capabilities, and basic ammunition
    descriptions are outlined within this appendix. The ammunition information
    provided is for training purposes.

AMMUNITION GUIDELINES

     A-1. The BFV crews complete the following events as indicated within this training
     circular. The actions listed below are in T-week (active component) and T-month
     (reserve component) concept. Frequency of execution of the training strategy is twice
     per fiscal year for active component and once per training year for reserve component.
     The sequence of the execution is the following:

          • Table I—GST is completed between T-6 and T-execution of a live-fire event.
          • Table II—Simulations is completed between T-6 and T-execution of a live-fire

                event, and each crew is authorized 1 hour per month with an authorized
                simulations system.
          • Table III—STX is completed between T-6 and T-execution of a live-fire event,
                and units should conduct Table III as concurrent training on all other listed
                tables.
          • Table IV—Basic is completed prior to Table V.
          • Table V—Practice is completed prior to Table VI.
          • Table VI—Qualification (duration is 12 months).

     A-2. The ammunition requirements may not match DA Pam 350-38 (also called
     STRAC or Standards in Training Commission), which is the authorization. (See
     figure A-1 on page 282 for the ammunition requirements for Tables III through VI.) The
     unit Master Gunner must determine the annual forecast of ammunition required to meet
     the training goals and objectives of the commander. The Master Gunner uses the Total
     Ammunition Management Information System to review, validate, and submit unit
     ammunition requirements and forecast their training ammunition authorizations by
     month.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  281

Appendix A

25-MM AMMUNITION COMPONENTS

     A-6. All 25-mm ammunition cartridges consist of a cartridge case and projectile
     assembly (see figure A-5). Within the cartridge case is the primer, booster pellet or flash
     tube (round dependent), and propellant. The projectile assembly contains the
     sub-projectile, windscreen, obturator, fuze, rotating band, ogive, and pusher base.

                        Figure A-5. 25-mm ammunition components

CARTRIDGE CASE

     A-7. The cartridge case is a steel casing that contains the primer, booster pellet or flash
     tube, and propellant. (See figure A-6 for detailed view of the case base.)

     Figure A-6. Cartridge case

286  TC 3-20.31-25               23 July 2025


---

                                                                                    Ammunition Requirements

Primer

     A-8. The primer (see figure A-7) is the cap in the base of the cartridge case. When
     struck by the firing pin, this starts the ignition sequence.

                                          Figure A-7. Primer

Flash Tube

     A-9. The flash tube (as shown in figure A-8), or the booster pellet (round dependent),
     is part of the ignition sequence of the ammunition. The flash tube or booster pellet is
     ignited by the primer, which in turn ignites the propellant in the cartridge case.

              Figure A-8. Flash tube

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25           287


---

Appendix A

Propellant

     A-10. The propellant is contained within the cartridge case (see figure A-9). The
     propellant is a composition that burns, producing gas pressure that forces the projectile
     assembly from the cartridge case down the gun barrel toward the target. The different
     propellant types vary by the model number of the round, listed later within this appendix.

                                        Figure A-9. Propellant

288  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                                    Ammunition Requirements

PROJECTILE ASSEMBLY

     A-11. The projectile is the cylindrical portion at the end of the cartridge case. The
     projectile travels down the bore toward the target. The projectile, based on the type of
     round, may house a sub projectile, windscreen, obturator, rotating band, fuze, ogive, and
     pusher base.
     A-12. Based on the type of round, after shot exit, the round may—

          • Arm a chemical warhead.
          • Begin separating sabot petals from a sub-projectile.
          • Continue down range on the ballistic path.
     A-13. The projectile has three different color applications: black, yellow, or light blue.
     The black projectile is an armor defeating round, yellow is for high explosive, and the
     light blue color indicates training (target practice). (See figure A-10 for an illustration
     of the projectile location.)

                                Figure A-10. Projectile assembly

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  289


---

Appendix A

Sub Projectile

     A-14. The sub projectile from a KE round is the portion of the round that travels to the
     target after it has discarded the sabots. (See figure A-11 for the sub projectile from a KE
     round.)

                                    Figure A-11. Sub projectile

Windscreen

     A-15. The windscreen is a pointed, or curved surface, mainly used to form the
     streamlined nose of the round. This is also known as the nose cap (see figure A-12).

     Figure A-12. Windscreen

290  TC 3-20.31-25            23 July 2025


---

                                                                                    Ammunition Requirements

Obturator

     A-16. The obturator is a rubber, nylon, or copper ring that seals propellant gases behind
     the projectile during firing. (See figure A-13 for a detailed view and location of the
     obturator.)

                                       Figure A-13. Obturator

Rotating Band

     A-17. The rotating band is an iron or nylon O-ring located on the projectile assembly
     which engages the rifling of the barrel and provides forward obturation. Forward
     obturation seals propellant gases behind the projectile during firing and causes the
     projectile assembly to spin. (See figure A-14 for a detailed view.)

              Figure A-14. Rotating band

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25               291


---

Appendix A

Fuze

     A-18. The fuze is part of the high explosive projectile that causes it to function upon
     impact with a target. This is not used with KE rounds. (See figure A-15 for a detailed
     view of the M758 fuze.)

     Figure A-15. Fuze

292  TC 3-20.31-25      23 July 2025


---

                                                                                    Ammunition Requirements

Ogive

     A-19. The ogive is on the end of the projectile assembly. This provides the aerodynamic
     shape which lowers the drag. (See figure A-16 for detailed view of the ogive location.)

                                          Figure A-16. Ogive

Pusher Base

     A-20. The pusher base is the metal base on certain projectile assemblies that propellant
     gases push against to drive the projectile assembly down the gun barrel. It is discarded
     from the projectile assembly once it leaves the gun barrel (see figure A-17).

              Figure A-17. Pusher base

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25             293

                                     Appendix B

         Tracking and Manipulation Boards

    T&MBs, or panels, are tools to train hand-and-eye, coordination, gun
    control, speed, and accuracy using the platform’s fire control system. In this
    appendix, there are six variations as examples for units to consider;
    however, units can create unique layouts, as their training program requires.
    T&MBs (more commonly referred to as snake boards) should have a wide
    variety of designs with horizontal, vertical, and oblique motion in any
    direction. The panels may also incorporate (along the panel’s tracking path)
    scaled targets that replicate the most effective way to lay on, lase, and
    engage a target. Paths on the boards can be tracked in all fire control modes
    and incorporate the use of all sights in most cases.

OVERVIEW–TRACKING AND MANIPULATION
BOARDS

     B-1. T&MB exercises are used to augment the STX-V or used when no simulator
     system is available or accessible to the crew. T&MBs are locally manufactured based
     on the unit need. Completion of the tracking board exercise is the least preferred method
     of completing Table II for live fire.

     B-2. It should only be used in extreme cases (new vehicle with no simulator system,
     unit is deployed, or retraining is necessary in an austere or field environment) or as
     opportunity training in the motor pool, training area, or admin area on a range facility.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  303


---

Appendix B

     B-3. The purpose of crew platform, Table II, STX-V, is to ensure the crew possesses
     the skills and experience necessary to execute live-fire events and exercises safely,
     which implies a functional understanding of the engagement process, fire commands,
     engagement techniques, and lethality. This is the culminating test of their abilities and
     skills on their platform in simulation after completing the appropriate simulations
     training plan (see figure B-1).

              Figure B-1. Table II, Situational Training Exercise–Virtual

     B-4. Table II, STX-V, is a mandatory prerequisite for any live-fire event, including
     zero procedures, sub-caliber or in-bore training event, or any live-fire maintenance
     procedure within the crew training strategy. Table II is completed between T-6 and
     T-week to support the training events.

     B-5. Units use the T&MBs when training in austere environments or when simulation
     systems are unavailable or unacceptable. This appendix provides general information on
     the purpose and use of the tracking and manipulation panels.

304  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                         Tracking and Manipulation Boards

PURPOSE–TRACKING AND MANIPULATION BOARDS

     B-6. Table II, STX-V, is a skills assessment and risk mitigation tool for the
     commander. It assists the commander in determining the safe and effective use of the
     platform, its weapons, identification of threats, and common ammunition by the
     crewmembers in the unit. Further, it identifies shortfalls and training gaps in those
     gunnery specific skills, which build crew proficiency and unit readiness.

METHOD–TRACKING AND MANIPULATION BOARDS

     B-7. Table II is a hands-on, performance-based evaluation of basic gunnery skills to
     the Army-standard of knowledge or skill in a simulator system (crew shelter). It utilizes
     experienced evaluators to educate, train, test, and retrain Soldiers to meet or exceed
     those standards.

END STATE–TRACKING AND MANIPULATION BOARDS

     B-8. The VC and gunner assigned as a crew have successfully demonstrated the skills
     and knowledge required to continue training on the platform through platform
     qualification. Commanders are confident in their Soldier’s abilities in the position
     assigned.

     B-9. The tasks evaluated in Table II provide the commander a means to certify the
     crew’s basic weapons platform proficiency prior to live-fire training. It is used as a guide
     for identifying individual and crew weapons platform strengths and weaknesses on
     specific engagement techniques and employment of the vehicle’s fire control system
     capabilities and enablers.

     B-10. The goal of this training is for the firer to reach the level of expertise with his or
     her system at which he or she anticipates movements of the sight and can maintain his
     or her point of aim while tracking. Examples of tracking boards are in appendix B with
     sample scripts for use and construction requirements. The tracking panel exercise
     increase the level of expertise of the firer to manipulate the weapon system rapidly and
     without hesitation, commonly referred to as switchology.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  305


---

Appendix B

TASK, CONDITIONS, AND STANDARD–TRACKING AND MANIPULATION
BOARDS

     B-11. Units maintain specific action, conditions, and standard when using the T&MBs
     for Table II (see figure B-2).

Figure B-2. Table II, Situational Training Exercise-tracking and manipulation
                                                 boards

306  TC 3-20.31-25  23 July 2025


---

                                                                         Tracking and Manipulation Boards

SCENARIO REQUIREMENTS–TRACKING AND
MANIPULATION BOARDS

     B-12. When using the T&MBs to meet Table II testing, units should use no less than 10
     separate movements to another panel across the array, complete the series of movements
     on each panel before assessing the gunner’s performance. As the layout, design, and
     functioning will vary, units must create their own timing and evaluation procedures.
     However, the standards listed in figure B-2 should not change.

     B-13. When used as the GTLF on Table II, the unit should maintain a record of the
     crew’s performance and evaluator notes. The method the unit records the training is up
     to the design and script developed for training. It should include information like the
     administrative data on DA Form 8265, including crew roster, evaluator name, and date
     of training, to name a few.

     B-14. Units should provide enough scorecards and performance measures for the
     evaluators. Maintain these records in the crew’s gunnery folder for 12 months.

DESIGN

     B-15. Platoon sergeants, the 1SG, or the unit Master Gunner will provide the VC
     training using the primary sight or independent thermal viewer as appropriate. Use of
     manual controls is not required for the VC’s exercise.

     B-16. The panels are constructed using 4-foot by 8-foot plywood, target cloth, or other
     suitable material. Tracking lines are 1-inch-wide black borders with a 2-inch-wide inner
     white path. Multiple panels can be aligned together by placing the start-point and
     endpoint of each panel at the height halfway point on the left and right side of the panel.

     B-17. The design of the panel track is up to the unit. (See example panel designs noted
     in figure B-3 on page 308 through B-9 on page 312.) Variations should include—

          • Vertical track.
          • Horizontal track.
          • Right oblique to left oblique (diagonal movement).
          • Left oblique to right oblique (diagonal movement).
          • Random directional changes.
          • Targetry that is removable, adjustable.
          • Switchology indicators that are removable, adjustable.

23 July 2025  TC 3-20.31-25  307

SCENARIO EXAMPLE

     B-21. In an example scenario using T&MBs (see figure B-13 on page 316 through

     and accuracy using power and manual controls, along with the primary and auxiliary

     indicators, they set up the training location. The panels are placed approximately

EXECUTION–TRACKING AND MANIPULATION
BOARDS

     B-24. Evaluators utilize the unit provided criteria scoring checklist for the 10 steps of
     the tracking and manipulation panel exercise. The evaluators will use specified prompts
     and evaluators may not assist the crew to perform any function.

     B-25. Once testing is complete, the evaluator records the results on a locally created
     roll-up form. As each Soldier finishes or the time limit is up, whichever occurs first, the
     evaluator checks the Soldier’s performance as either GO or NO-GO, informs them of
     their performance on that task, and directs them to the next station or to retraining.

     headquarters is informed, if required.
